Homology of configuration spaces of surfaces modulo an odd prime
Andrea Bianchi, Andreas Stavrou

Abstract
. For a compact orientable surface Σ g , 1 \Sigma_{g,1} of genus g g with one boundary component and for an odd prime number p p , we study the homology of the unordered configuration spaces C ∙ ​ ( Σ g , 1 ) := ∐ n ≥ 0 C n ​ ( Σ g , 1 ) C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1}):=\coprod_{n\geq 0}C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1}) with coefficients in 𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p} . We describe H ∗ ​ ( C ∙ ​ ( Σ g , 1 ) , 𝔽 p ) H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p}) as a bigraded module over the Pontryagin ring H ∗ ​ ( C ∙ ​ ( D ) , 𝔽 p ) H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{p}) , where D D is a disc, and compute in particular the bigraded dimension over 𝔽 p \mathbb{F}_{p} . We also consider the action of the mapping class group Γ g , 1 \Gamma_{g,1} , and prove that the mod- p p Johnson kernel 𝒦 g , 1 ​ ( p ) ⊆ Γ g , 1 \mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)\subseteq\Gamma_{g,1} is the kernel of the action on H ∗ ​ ( C ∙ ​ ( Σ g , 1 , 𝔽 p ) ) H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1};\mathbb{F}_{p})) .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

16E30, 20F36, 55R35, 55R80, 55U25

1.
Introduction and statement of results

We are interested in the homology of configuration spaces of surfaces, as plain abelian groups and as representations of the mapping class group.
For a topological space
X
and
n\geq 0
, the
n
th

unordered
configuration space of
X
, denoted
C_{n}(X)
, is defined as the quotient

C_{n}(X)=\mathopen{}\left\{(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\in X^{n}\,|\,p_{i}\neq p_{j}\mbox{ forall }i\neq j\right\}\mathclose{}\,/\,\mathfrak{S}_{n},

where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts on the space of
n
-tuples of distinct points by relabeling them.

We denote by
\Sigma_{g,1}
a compact, connected, orientable surface of genus
g\geq 0
with one boundary curve. The group
\mathrm{Homeo}(\Sigma_{g,1},\partial\Sigma_{g,1})

of homeomorphisms of the surface
\Sigma_{g,1}
fixing the boundary pointwise acts on
C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})
, and hence on the homology
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
with coefficients in a commutative ring
R
; the latter action descends to an action of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,1}:=\pi_{0}\mathrm{Homeo}(\Sigma_{g,1},\partial\Sigma_{g,1})
on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
.
We are interested in the following two questions.

Question 1.1
.

What
R
-module is
H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
? Specifically, if
R
is a field, what is the dimension of
H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
over
R
?

Question 1.2
.

How does the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,1}
act on
H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
? What is the kernel of this action?

For
R
being
\mathbb{F}_{2}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, the first question is answered in
[
5
]
and
[
4
,
11
]
, respectively, and an answer to both questions can be found in
[
2
]
and
[
27
]
, respectively.
In this paper we will mainly focus on the cases
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, for
p
an odd prime.

1.1.
Mapping class group action

Our first main result is the following.

Theorem A
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
R
be a commutative ring.

(1)

The Johnson kernel
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}\subseteq\Gamma_{g,1}
, i.e. the subgroup generated by separating Dehn twists, acts trivially on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
.

(2)

For
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
R=\mathbb{Q}
, the kernel of the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
is the Johnson kernel.

(3)

For
p
an odd prime, the kernel of the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is the mod-
p
Johnson kernel
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)\subseteq\Gamma_{g,1}
, i.e. the subgroup generated by separating Dehn twists and
p
th
powers of all Dehn twists.

For
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the kernel of the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(2)

[
2
]
. The fact that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
already appears in
[
27
]
.

1.2.
Homology of configurations as Ext groups

Let
R
be a commutative ring.
Our second, main result, Theorem
B
, describes the direct sum

H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)

as a tensor product of simpler terms, and in particular as a module over the Pontryagin ring

H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);R)\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{*}(C_{n}(D);R),

where
D\cong\Sigma_{0,1}
is a disc. Here we use that
C_{\bullet}(D)=\coprod_{n\geq 0}C_{n}(D)
has an
E_{2}
-algebra structure induced by embedding several copies of
D
inside
D
, and that
C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1})=\coprod_{n\geq 0}C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})
has a structure of
E_{1}
-module over
C_{\bullet}(D)
induced by identifying
\Sigma_{g,1}\cong\Sigma_{g,1}\cup_{\frac{1}{2}\partial}D
and by embedding several copies of
D
in
D\subset\Sigma_{g,1}
.

In Theorem
B
we make use of the bigrading
(\bullet,\star)
given by the two gradings
\bullet
(called
weight
) and
\star
(called
bar-degree
); the grading
*
can be recovered as
\bullet+\star
.

We work in the abelian category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of weighted
R
-modules, i.e. of
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed families of
R
-modules, endowed with the tensor product given by Day convolution with respect to sum in
\mathbb{Z}
and tensor product of
R
-modules.
We let
\Gamma_{R}(y)
denote the free divided power
R
-algebra generated by a variable
y
of weight
-2
(the negative weight of
y
ensures that the terms in Theorem
B
are in non-negative weight).
We let
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}
denote a free
R
-module of rank
2g
concentrated in bigrading
(\bullet,\star)=(2,0)
, and we let
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}]
denote the free symmetric
R
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}
; we consider
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}]
just as a bigraded
R
-module.
Finally, in Definition
6.7
we introduce, for
g\geq 0
, an explicit
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}
: we remark here that
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}
is concentrated in non-positive weights and is finitely generated and free over
R
.

Theorem B
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring, and set
*=\bullet+\star
.

(1)

There is an isomorphism of bigraded rings

H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);R)\cong R[\epsilon]\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R).

Here
R[\epsilon]
is the polynomial ring in one variable
\epsilon
in bigrading
(1,-1)
, and we endow
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R)
with the Yoneda product.

(2)

There is an isomorphism of bigraded

R[\epsilon]\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R)
-modules

H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)\cong R[\epsilon]\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)\otimes_{R}R[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}].

Here
R[\epsilon]
acts on itself by ring multiplication, whereas
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R)
acts by the Yoneda product on
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)
.

Part (1) of Theorem
B
, in the stated form, was first proved by Markaryan
[
21
]
, and is preceded by classical computations of
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);R)
as bigraded vector spaces for
R
being a field of characteristic zero
[
1
]
, two
[
14
]
or an odd prime
p

[
28
]
, and in all characteristics a description of the ring structure as well as of the action of the dual Steenrod algebra is given in
[
9
, Chapter III]
. Similar results comparing twisted homology of braid groups and cohomology of augmented algebras can be found in
[
7
,
12
,
15
]
.
For
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, Theorem
B
recovers computations of
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
already present in the literature
[
5
,
2
,
4
,
11
,
27
]
.

Remark 1.3
.

In Theorem
B
, and later in Theorem
C
,
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(-,R)
is the collection of the right derived functors of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(-,R)
. The latter is
part of the internal hom-functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(-,-)
of the abelian category
\mathrm{Mod}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\mathbb{Z}})
of weighted
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules in
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, endowed with the monoidal structure of weighted tensor product over
\Gamma_{R}(y)
.
We adopt the convention that
\mathrm{Ext}
-groups are concentrated in bar-degree
\star\leq 0
. So we denote by
\mathrm{Ext}^{0}
the functor
\mathrm{Hom}
, by
\mathrm{Ext}^{-1}
the first right derived functor of
\mathrm{Hom}
, and so on.

Theorem
B
recovers the following results due to
[
22
,
26
]
(see also
[
25
]
), and due to
[
19
]
(also implicit in
[
4
,
11
,
27
]
).

Corollary B.1

(Split homological stability and rational extremal stability)
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

The map
\epsilon\cdot-\colon H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)\to H_{i}(C_{n+1}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
is split injective and is an isomorphism for
i\leq\frac{1}{2}n
.

(2)

For
i\geq 0
, there are two polynomials
P_{g,i}^{1}(s),P_{g,i}^{-1}(s)\in\mathbb{Q}[s]
of degree
2g-1
such that for all
n\geq 2g+i+1
there is an equality

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H_{n-i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})=P_{g,i}^{(-1)^{n}}(n).

For an original corollary of Theorem
B
, note that for
g,g^{\prime}\geq 0
we can embed the disjoint union
\Sigma_{g,1}\sqcup\Sigma_{g^{\prime},1}
inside
\Sigma_{g+g^{\prime},1}
as the complement of a pair of pants, and for all
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
we obtain an inclusion

C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})\times C_{n^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{g^{\prime},1})\hookrightarrow C_{n+n^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{g+g^{\prime},1}).

We now set
g^{\prime}=n^{\prime}=1
.

Corollary B.2

(Filled genus stabilisation)
.

Let
g,n\geq 0
. Then the embedding construction induces a split injective map

H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)\otimes_{R}H_{1}(\Sigma_{1,1};R)\hookrightarrow H_{*+1}(C_{n+1}(\Sigma_{g+1,1});R).

We remark that, instead, the plain genus stabilisation map

H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)\to H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g+1,1});R)

is not injective in general; for instance one can identify the map

H_{1}(C_{2}(\Sigma_{0,1});\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(C_{2}(\Sigma_{1,1});\mathbb{Z})

with the composite map
\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

1.3.
Computation of the Ext groups

We next move to our third, main result of the paper. Theorem
B

reduces the computation of
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
to the computation of
the ring
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R)
and of its modules

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)
.

Let now
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
p
an odd prime.
Setting
\mathfrak{y}_{i}=y^{[i]}=y^{i}/i!
, we have that
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
is isomorphic as a weighted algebra to the truncated polynomial algebra

\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2},\dots]/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{p},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{p}\dots),

and consequently we have an isomorphism of bigraded algebras

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\bigotimes_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\bigotimes_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}],

where

\alpha_{i}\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\ \mbox{ and }\ \beta_{i}\in\mathrm{Ext}^{2}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})

are generators, and
\bigotimes_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]=\mathbb{F}_{p}[\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\dots]

denotes the free graded-commutative algebra generated by the classes
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\dots
.

We postpone the statement of Theorem
C
to the body of the paper, due to its technical nature, but we briefly discuss its content here.
In Definition
6.9
we introduce for all
u\geq 0
a certain weighted
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
-module
B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u}
, and in Proposition
6.11
we decompose the
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
-module
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
as a finite direct sum of weighted shifts of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
-modules of the form
B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u}
. We thus reduce the computation of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}},\mathbb{F}_{p})
to the computation of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u},\mathbb{F}_{p})
, and the latter is achieved in Theorem
C
.

The following corollaries follow from Theorem
C
and its argument of proof.

Corollary C.1

(Qualitative module structure)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime, and let
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}]
be as in Theorem
B
; then
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic, as a module over
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[\epsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\dots]
, to a finite direct sum of modules of the following forms, where
i\geq 0
(tensor products are taken here over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
):

\mathbb{F}_{p}[\epsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\dots]/(\alpha_{0},\dots,\beta_{i-1})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}];\quad\mathbb{F}_{p}[\epsilon,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\dots]/(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{i})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}].

Corollary C.2

(Near top degree generators)
.

Let
g\geq 0
and let
p
be an odd prime; then
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is generated as a
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{p})
-module by the direct sum

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{n-1}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{n}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p}).

The same result holds with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
or
\mathbb{Q}
; in fact
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a free
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{2})
-module generated by the vector space
\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{n}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{2})
, see
[
2
]
.
The terminology “near top” alludes to the vanishing of
H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
for
i>n
, as
C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})
is homotopy equivalent to a complex of dimension at most
n
.

Corollary C.3

(Genus versus prime)
.

Let
g\geq 0
,
\lambda\geq 0
, and let
p
be an odd prime.

(1)

if
g<p^{\lambda}
, then
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is free as a module over the subring

\mathbb{F}_{p}[\epsilon,\alpha_{\lambda},\beta_{\lambda},\alpha_{\lambda+1},\beta_{\lambda+1},\dots]\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}]

of the ring
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}]
;

(2)

if
g<(p-1)p^{\lambda-1}
, then
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is free as a module over the subring

\mathbb{F}_{p}[\epsilon,\beta_{\lambda-1},\alpha_{\lambda},\beta_{\lambda},\alpha_{\lambda+1},\dots]\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathcal{H}_{g}].

Corollary C.4

(Top and top minus 1 homology)
.

Let
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
; then
H_{n}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
is a free abelian group, whereas
H_{n-1}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
has only
2
-power torsion.
As a consequence, for
p
an odd prime we have

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(H_{n}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q}))=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(H_{n}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})).

Corollary C.5

(
p
-power torsion)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime and
n\geq 0
; then the
p
-power torsion part of
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
coincides with the image of the Bockstein homomorphism

H_{*+1}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})

induced by the short exact sequence
\mathbb{Z}\overset{\cdot p}{\to}\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}
; in particular all
p
-power torsion classes are in fact
p
-torsion.

Corollary C.6

(Fake basechange)
.

For a bigraded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
V
and for an odd prime
p
let
V^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
be a bigraded
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-vector space having in each bigrading the same dimension (over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
) as
V
(over
\mathbb{Q}
). Then for
g\leq p-2
there is an isomorphism of bigraded
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-vector spaces

H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\mathopen{}\left(H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})\right)\mathclose{}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}[\beta_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\dots].

Corollary C.7

(Low dimensional Betti numbers)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime and
g,n\geq 0
. Then for
i<\max\mathopen{}\left\{2p-2,g+p\right\}\mathclose{}
,
H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
has no
p
-torsion. Equivalently, for
i<\max\mathopen{}\left\{2p-2,g+p\right\}\mathclose{}
we have

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(H_{i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})).

Corollaries
C.4
and
C.7
imply that
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
has no
p
-torsion for
n<\max\mathopen{}\left\{2p,g+p+2\right\}\mathclose{}
. This partially confirms the prediction of
[
8
, Remark 3.7]
.

Corollary C.8

(Extremal asymptotic growth)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime, and let
g\geq 1
and
i\geq 0
. Then there exist constants
0<\mathbf{c}_{g,i}<\mathbf{C}_{g,i}
such that, for
n\geq i
,

\mathbf{c}_{g,i}\log_{p}(n)^{i}n^{2g-1}<\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}H_{n-i}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})<\mathbf{C}_{g,i}\log_{p}(n)^{i}n^{2g-1}.

1.4.
Related work

Configuration spaces of manifolds
M
and their homology are a classical topic in algebraic topology. Arnold
[
1
]
, Fuchs
[
14
]
and Weinstein
[
28
]
computed
H_{*}(C_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{2});R)
for
R
being
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}
respectively. The computation of
H_{*}(C_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{d});R)
for any ring
R
is due to Cohen
[
9
, Chapter III]
.

Bödigheimer–Cohen–Taylor
[
5
]
compute explicitly
H_{*}(C_{n}(M);R)
, as a bigraded vector space, for
d\geq 3
odd and any field
R
, and for
d\geq 2
and
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
: in the case
M=\Sigma_{g,1}
, their result only settles the case
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
. The first computation of
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
as a graded vector space is due to Bödigheimer–Cohen
[
4
]
; a computation of the rational cohomology of
C_{n}(M)
, for
M
any compact, connected, possibly non-orientable surface, is given in
[
11
]
. A description of
H_{*}(C_{n}(M);\mathbb{Q})
for a generic even-dimensional manifold
M
was given by Félix–Thomas
[
13
]
and by Knudsen
[
17
]
, and a detailed computation of rational Betti numbers in the case of surfaces was done by Drummond-Cole–Knudsen
[
11
]
.

For a generic even-dimensional manifold
M
and an odd prime
p
, very little is known about
H_{*}(C_{n}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
. To the best of our knowledge, this article contains the first complete computation for an orientable, even-dimensional manifold
M
which is not an open subset of another manifold of the form
M^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}
.

We mention that Brantner–Hahn–Knudsen
[
6
, Corollary 1.11]
have computed
H_{*}(C_{p}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a bigraded vector space, obtaining the special case of Corollary
C.7
for
n=p
. Their strategy is to first consider the Morava
E
-theory of
p
at height
h\geq 0
, denoted
E_{h}
, and compute
E^{*}_{h}(C_{p}(\Sigma_{g,1}))
; then deduce a computation of the Morava
K
-theory
K(h)^{*}(C_{p}(\Sigma_{g,1}))
; and finally deduce a computation of
H^{*}(C_{p}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
by observing that, for
h
large enough, the
K(h)
-based Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence collapses. A simplified argument was then given by Chen–Zhang
[
8
]
, building on
[
30
,
18
]
.
The methods used in this paper are more elementary, and whether they can be applied to the computation of other cohomology theories on configuration spaces of surfaces seems to be a difficult question.

The action of the mapping class group on the homology of
ordered
configuration spaces was treated in
[
3
]
, building on work of Moriyama
[
23
]
; in the unordered case, the problem has been studied with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients by the first author
[
2
]
, and with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients by Looijenga
[
20
]
and by the second author
[
27
]
.

1.5.

Part I
The action of the Johnson filtration

2.
Partially compactified configurations

In this paper, we work in the category of compactly generated Hausdorff spaces.
Let
(X,X^{\prime})
be a pair of spaces with
X^{\prime}\subset X
closed. For
n\geq 0
we define the following two closed subspaces of
X^{n}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}(X)

\displaystyle:=\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in X^{n}\,|\,x_{i}=x_{j}\text{ for some }i\neq j\},

\displaystyle A_{n}(X,X^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in X^{n}\,|\,x_{i}\in X^{\prime}\text{ for some }i\}.

Any continuous map
f:X\to Y
induces a map
f^{n}\colon X^{n}\to Y^{n}
that restricts to a map

\Delta_{n}(f)\colon\Delta_{n}(X)\to\Delta_{n}(Y).

If
f
is moreover a map of pairs
(X,X^{\prime})\to(Y,Y^{\prime})
, then
f^{n}
also restricts to a map

A_{n}(f)\colon A_{n}(X,X^{\prime})\to A_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime}).

Finally, the permutation action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
X^{n}
preserves the subsets
\Delta_{n}(X)
and
A_{n}(X,X^{\prime})
.

Definition 2.1
.

For
n\geq 1
and a pair of spaces
(X,X^{\prime})
, we define the quotient

{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}:=\big(X^{n}/(\Delta_{n}(X)\cup A_{n}(X,X^{\prime}))\big)/\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

We denote by
\infty
the image of
\Delta_{n}(X)\cup A_{n}(X,X^{\prime})
in the quotient,
which serves as basepoint for
{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
. For
n=0
, we define
{C}_{0}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
to be the discrete space
\{\infty,\emptyset\}
based at
\infty
. We write the wedge of pointed spaces
{C}_{\bullet}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}:=\bigvee_{n\geq 0}{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
.

The image of
(x_{1},...,x_{n})
in
{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
is denoted by
\left[x_{1},...,x_{n}\right]
; we often denote by
s
a generic point in
{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
.

In the case of a pointed space
(X,x_{0})
, we also write
\widetilde{C}_{n}(X)^{\infty}:={C}_{n}(X,\{x_{0}\})^{\infty}
, and
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(X)^{\infty}={C}_{\bullet}(X,\{x_{0}\})^{\infty}
.

Observe that
{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
contains
C_{n}(X\setminus X^{\prime})
and, if
X
is compact, then
{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
is also compact; this justifies the terminology of Definition
2.1
.
Furthermore,

{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
is homeomorphic to
\widetilde{C}_{n}(X/X^{\prime})^{\infty}
, where the basepoint of
X/X^{\prime}
is the image of
X^{\prime}
. A map of pairs
f:(X,X^{\prime})\to(Y,Y^{\prime})
induces a map
{C}_{n}(f)^{\infty}:{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}\to{C}_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})^{\infty}
.

2.1.
Connection to
C_{n}(M)

We let
M
be a compact manifold with boundary throughout the subsection.

Notation 2.2
.

We will use the shorthand
{C}_{n}(M)^{\infty}:={C}_{n}(M,\partial M)^{\infty}
, which coincides with the one-point compactification of
C_{n}(\mathring{M})
, where
\mathring{M}\subseteq M
is the interior of
M
.

The group
\mathrm{Homeo}(M,\partial M)
of orientation-preserving homeomorphisms of
M
fixing
\partial M
pointwise acts on both
C_{n}(\mathring{M})
and
{C}_{n}(M)^{\infty}
by homeomorphisms.

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
M
is oriented and of even dimension
2d
. Then for every
n,i\geq 0
and every commutative ring
R
, there is a
\mathrm{Homeo}(M,\partial M)
-equivariant
1
1

1

Strictly speaking, we assume that
\mathrm{Homeo}(M,\partial M)
acts via the adjoint action on one of the two
R
-modules.
isomorphism
H_{i}(C_{n}(M);R)\cong\widetilde{H}^{dn-i}({C}_{n}(M)^{\infty};R)
.

Proof.

A standard application of Poincaré-Lefschetz duality.
∎

Guided by this proposition, in Sections
3
and
4
we compute a cellular chain complex for
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty}
, admitting an action of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,1}:=\pi_{0}\mathrm{Homeo}(\Sigma_{g,1},\partial\Sigma_{g,1})
on it.

2.2.
Superposition of configurations

Definition 2.4
.

For
m,n\geq 0
and for a pair of spaces
(X,X^{\prime})
we define the map

\displaystyle\mu_{m,n}:{C}_{m}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}\times{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}

\displaystyle\to{C}_{m+n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}

\displaystyle(\left[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\right],\left[y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\right])

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\right].

Observe that
\mu_{m,n}(s,s^{\prime})=\infty
if either
s
or
s^{\prime}
is
\infty
, and that
\mu_{n,0}(s,\emptyset)=\mu_{0,n}(\emptyset,s)=s
and
\mu_{n,0}(s,\infty)=\mu_{0,n}(\infty,s)=\infty
.

We let
\mu\colon{C}_{\bullet}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}\times{C}_{\bullet}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}\to{C}_{\bullet}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
be the map restricting to
\mu_{m,n}
on
{C}_{m}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}\times{C}_{n}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty}
.

Remark 2.5
.

It follows that the pair
\big({C}_{\bullet}(X,X^{\prime})^{\infty},\mu\big)
is an abelian topological monoid with unit
\emptyset
and absorbing element
\infty
.

3.
Configurations on bouquets of circles

We introduce a model for bouquets of circles. The setup of this section is analogous to
[
23
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
k\geq 1
, construct the bouquet of
k
circles as the quotient

V_{k}:=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}[0,1]\times\{i\}/\sim

where
\sim
collapses the endpoints of all intervals to a single point
*
. Picking the standard orientation on
[0,1]
, we orient each circle in
V_{k}\cong\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}S^{1}
. We let
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}\in\pi_{1}(V_{k},*)
be the standard generators, where
\gamma_{i}
is represented by the loops
t\mapsto[t,i]
. We use the elements
\gamma_{i}
to identify
\pi_{1}(V_{k},*)
with the standard, free group
\mathbb{Z}^{*k}
on
k
generators.

3.1.
A cell stratification for
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}

For
n\geq 0
, our model for the standard simplex
\Delta^{n}
is

\Delta^{n}=\{(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\dots\leq t_{n}\leq 1\},

oriented as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
k\geq 1
. For a vector
\mathfrak{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times k}
, we define

•

d(\mathfrak{v})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}
, the
dimension
of
\mathfrak{v}
,

•

the polysimplex
\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Delta^{v_{i}}
,

•

and the map
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}:\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}\to\widetilde{C}_{d(\mathfrak{v})}(V_{k})^{\infty}
, with

\displaystyle\Big((t^{(1)}_{1},

\displaystyle\dots,t^{(1)}_{v_{1}}),(t^{(2)}_{1},\dots,t^{(2)}_{v_{2}}),\dots,(t^{(k)}_{1},\dots,t^{(k)}_{v_{k}})\Big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[(t^{(1)}_{1},1),\dots,(t^{(1)}_{v_{1}},1),(t^{(2)}_{1},2),\dots,(t^{(2)}_{v_{2}},2),\dots,(t^{(k)}_{1},k),\dots,(t^{(k)}_{v_{k}},k)\right].

The image of
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}
is the space of configurations on
V_{k}
with precisely
v_{i}
points on the
i
th
circle, for
1\leq i\leq k
: more precisely,
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}
restricts to a topological embedding of the interior of
\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}
, with image the subspace
\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{v_{i}}((0,1)\times\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{})\subset\widetilde{C}_{d(\mathfrak{v})}(V_{k})^{\infty}
, and it maps
\partial\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}
constantly to the point
\infty\in\widetilde{C}_{d(\mathfrak{v})}(V_{k})^{\infty}
.

Conversely, for all
n\geq 0
, each configuration
s\in\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}\setminus\mathopen{}\left\{\infty\right\}\mathclose{}
lies in the image of
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}
for exactly one vector
\mathfrak{v}
with
d(\mathfrak{v})=n
.
We obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

The space
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}
admits a cellular decomposition with

•

a
0
-cell given by the point
\infty
;

•

for each
\mathfrak{v}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times k}
, a
d(\mathfrak{v})
-dimensional open cell
e_{\mathfrak{v}}:=\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}(\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{v}})
, whose boundary is attached to the
0
-cell
\infty
. For
\mathfrak{v}=0
we get a further
0
-cell.

In particular, for
n\geq 0
, the subspace
\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}\subset\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}
is homeomorphic to the wedge of
\binom{n+k-1}{k-1}

spheres of dimension
n
.

We next consider the cellular chain complex of
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}
.

Definition 3.4
.

We denote the reduced
2
2

2

that is, relative to the
0
-cell corresponding to
\infty
.
cellular chain complex corresponding to the cellular decomposition from Proposition
3.3
by

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k):=\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left(\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{};

we view
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
as a
weighted
chain complex, and in particular as a weighted abelian group, in the following way: for
n\geq 0
we define the summand
\mathrm{UMor}_{-n}(k)
as
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
, which in fact only consists of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{n}\mathopen{}\left(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
; the summand
\mathrm{UMor}_{-n}(k)
is in non-positive weight
\bullet=-n
.

The previous definition is a version of a construction of Moriyama
[
23
]
, in the context of unordered configuration spaces: this explains the notation “
\mathrm{UMor}
”.
A direct consequence of Proposition
3.3
is that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
has zero differential and is a free abelian group generated by the cells
e_{\mathfrak{v}}
, for varying

\mathfrak{v}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times k}
.
Therefore
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
is also isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{H}_{*}(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty})\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{H}_{n}(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}).

The reason why we put
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2k)
in non-positive weights will become clear when approaching the proof of Theorem
B
. For the moment, observe that by Poincaré duality the homology group

\widetilde{H}_{n}(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty})
is isomorphic to the
cohomology
group
H^{0}(C_{n}(\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}\mathclose{}\times(0,1)))
: this is the dual of
H_{0}(C_{n}(\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}\mathclose{}\times(0,1)))
, and our convention puts the latter in weight
n
, and the former in weight
-n
.

For
1\leq i\leq k
let now
\mathfrak{e}_{i}=(0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times k}

be the unit vector in the
i
th
direction,
and write
e(i,n):=e_{n\mathfrak{e}_{i}}
for the cell corresponding to the vector
n\mathfrak{e}_{i}
, parametrising configurations of
n
points, all lying on
(0,1)\times\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{}
.

Definition 3.5
.

For
m,n\geq 0
, a
shuffle
of type
(m,n)
is a permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
that is increasing on the sets
\{1,...,m\}
and
\{m+1,...,m+n\}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{Shuf}_{m,n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
the set of all shuffles of type
(m,n)
.

For
m,n\geq 0
the
signed shuffle
coefficient is

\mathbf{ss}(m,n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{if both }m,n\text{ are odd,}\\
\binom{\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}{\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor}=\binom{\lfloor\frac{m+n}{2}\rfloor}{\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor}&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
m,n\geq 0
we have that
\mathbf{ss}(m,n)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{Shuf}_{m,n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)
.

Proof.

This is a double induction on
m,n\geq 0
and is left to the reader.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

For
m,n\geq 0
we define a map of pairs

\begin{split}\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{m,n}\colon(\Delta^{m}\times\Delta^{n},\partial(\Delta^{m}\times\Delta^{n}))&\to(\Delta^{m+n},\partial\Delta^{m+n})\\
((s_{1},\dots,s_{m}),(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))&\mapsto(p_{1},\dots,p_{m+n}),\end{split}

where
(p_{1},\dots,p_{m+n})
is the weakly increasing sorting of the sequence of real numbers
s_{1},\dots,s_{m},t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
, repeated with multiplicity.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
m,n\geq 0
, the degree of
\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{m,n}
is equal to
\mathbf{ss}(m,n)
.

Proof.

This is a combinatorial exercise left to the reader.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

The product
\mu
from Definition
2.4
endows
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
with a ring structure.
By setting
x_{i}:=e(i,1)
and
y_{i}:=e(i,2)
, we have the isomorphism of weighted rings

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\otimes\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})

where
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}
denote, respectively, the free exterior algebra and the free divided power algebra, where the elements
x_{i}
have weight
\bullet=-1
and the elements
y_{i}
have weight
\bullet=-2
.
In particular, a homomorphism of rings with source
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
is characterised by its values on the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,k
, if the target ring is torsion-free as an abelian group.

Proof.

We check that for
m,n\geq 0
the map
\mu_{m,n}
from Definition
2.4
is cellular. The cells of
\widetilde{C}_{n+m}(V_{k})^{\infty}
are in dimension
0
and
(m+n)
, and
\widetilde{C}_{m}(V_{k})^{\infty}\times\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}
has product cells of dimension
0,m,n
and
(m+n)
. The union of all
0
-cells,
m
-cells and
n
-cells in the product
\widetilde{C}_{m}(V_{k})^{\infty}\times\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}
consists of all pairs
(s_{1},s_{2})
in which at least one between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
is
\infty
; for such pairs we have
\mu_{m,n}(s_{1},s_{2})=\infty
, which is a
0
-cell. So
\mu_{m,n}
preserves skeleta, i.e. it is cellular. Thus
\mu
is cellular and induces a map
\mu_{*}
that makes
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
into a bigraded ring, and similarly, making
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
into a weighted ring (concentrated in non-positive weights).

By the definition of the map
\Phi_{\mathfrak{v}}
, all points in the image of
e_{\mathfrak{v}}\times e_{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
along
\mu_{m,n}
are contained in the union
\mathopen{}\left\{\infty\right\}\mathclose{}\cup e_{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
, thus
e_{\mathfrak{v}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}=\lambda(\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime})e_{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
for some
\lambda(\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{v}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}
. Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{v}
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}
have disjoint supports, then up to a permutation of the
k
coordinates in
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\times k}
the polysimplex
\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
can be identified with
\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}\times\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
, and
\Phi^{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
coincides with
\mu\circ(\Phi^{\mathfrak{v}}\times\Phi^{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}})
. Thus
e_{\mathfrak{v}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}=\pm e_{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
; the sign depends on whether or not the identification
\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}+\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}\cong\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}}\times\Delta^{\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}}
is orientation-preserving. We deduce that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
is generated as a ring by the elements
e(i,n)
for
i=1,\dots,k
and
n\geq 1
.

A particular case of the previous discussion is when
\mathfrak{v}=m\mathfrak{e}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{v}^{\prime}=n\mathfrak{e}_{j}
for some
1\leq i,j\leq k
with
i\neq j
: then the sign
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
e(i,m)\cdot e(j,n)=\epsilon\hskip 2.0pte(j,n)\cdot e(i,m)
is equal to the degree of the map
\Delta^{m}\times\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
swapping the two coordinates, i.e.
\epsilon=(-1)^{mn}
.

We next study the value of the coefficient
\lambda(m\mathfrak{e}_{i},n\mathfrak{e}_{i})
for a fixed
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, and show that
\lambda(m\mathfrak{e}_{i},n\mathfrak{e}_{i})
is equal to
\mathbf{ss}(m,n)
.
For
l\geq 0
, write
\Phi^{l}
for the map

\Phi^{l\mathfrak{e}_{i}}/\partial\Delta^{l}\colon\Delta^{l}/\partial\Delta^{l}\overset{\cong}{\to}\widetilde{C}_{l}(S^{1}\times\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{})^{\infty}\subset\widetilde{C}_{l}(V_{k})^{\infty},

induced by
\Phi^{l\mathfrak{e}_{i}}
on the quotient: this map sends the interior of
\Delta^{l}
homeomorphically onto
C_{l}((0,1)\times\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{})\subset\widetilde{C}_{l}(V_{k})^{\infty}
.

The following diagram of maps of pairs is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta^{m}\times\Delta^{n},\partial(\Delta^{m}\times\Delta^{n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{C}_{m}(V_{k})^{\infty},\infty)\times(\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty},\infty)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta^{m+n},\partial\Delta^{m+n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{C}_{m+n}(V_{k})^{\infty},\infty);}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{m}\times\Phi^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{m,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{m,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{n+m}}

since the bottom map is the characteristic map of a cell, we have that
\lambda(m\mathfrak{e}_{i},n\mathfrak{e}_{i})
is equal to the degree of
\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{m,n}
, which is
\mathbf{ss}(m,n)
by Lemma
3.8
.

We conclude that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
is generated as a ring by the elements
e(i,n)
for
i=1,\dots,k
and
n\geq 1
(the elements
e(i,0)
are equal to each other and give the unit of the ring); since
\mathbf{ss}(2m,1)=1
for all
m\geq 0
, we can in fact factor
e(i,2m+1)=e(i,2m)e(2i,1)
, so that the elements
e(i,1)
and
e(i,2m)
also generate
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
.

The above discussion shows that the elements
e(i,1)
anticommute, the elements
e(i,2m)
are central, and
e(i,2m)\cdot e(i,2n)=\binom{m+n}{m}e(i,2(m+n))
. Therefore
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
receives a natural homomorphism of rings with source the tensor product of
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras

\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\otimes\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}),

mapping
x_{i}\mapsto e(i,1)
and
y_{i}\mapsto e(i,2)
, and the above discussion shows that this is in fact a bijection of rings.
∎

3.2.
Contents

Let
G_{k}:=\pi_{1}(V_{k},*)=\langle\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}^{*k}
as in Definition
3.1
. Write
H_{k}:=(G_{k})^{\mathrm{ab}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
[-]:G_{k}\to H_{k}
for the abelianisation map. Observe that in the exterior algebra
\Lambda H_{k}
the element
1+[\gamma_{i}]
has multiplicative inverse
1-[\gamma_{i}]
.

Definition 3.10
.

The
content
is the ring homomorphism
c:\mathbb{Z}[G_{k}]\longrightarrow\Lambda H_{k}
defined on the ring generators as
\gamma_{i}\mapsto 1+[\gamma_{i}]
and
\gamma_{i}^{-1}\mapsto 1-[\gamma_{i}]
. Write
c_{i}
for the component of
c
in the
i
th
exterior power
\Lambda^{i}H_{k}
; note that
c_{i}\colon\mathbb{Z}[G_{k}]\to\Lambda^{i}H_{k}
is a homomorphism of abelian groups. (See
[
27
]
for a similar construction.)

Lemma 3.11
.

The unique function of sets
C:G_{k}\to\Lambda^{2}H_{k}
satisfying that

(1)

C
vanishes on the generators
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
, and

(2)

C(w_{1}w_{2})=C(w_{1})+C(w_{2})+\left[w_{1}\right]\wedge\left[w_{2}\right]
for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in G_{k}
,

is the restriction of
c_{2}
to
G_{k}\subset\mathbb{Z}[G_{k}]
.

Proof.

The extension of
C
to the function

\bar{C}:=1+[-]+C:G_{k}\to\Lambda^{*}H_{k}/\Lambda^{\geq 3}H_{k}

is multiplicative by property (2). As a result,
\bar{C}
is defined by its values on the generators of
G_{k}
, on which, by property (1), it takes the value
\bar{c}(\gamma_{i})=1+[\gamma_{i}]
. Therefore
\bar{C}
agrees with
c
modulo
\Lambda^{\geq 3}H_{k}
and therefore
C=c_{2}
.
∎

3.3.
Induced maps on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}

A based map
f:(V_{k},*)\to(V_{l},*)
induces a map
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f):\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)\to\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(l)
which depends on
f
only up to based homotopy. So, by abuse of notation, for a homomorphism
\phi:G_{k}\to G_{l}
, we write
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\phi):=\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f)
, where
f\colon V_{k}\to V_{l}
is any based map inducing
\phi
on
\pi_{1}
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{UMor}_{*}(-)
is
functorial
, that is for composable homomorphisms
\phi:G_{k}\to G_{l}
and
\psi:G_{l}\to G_{m}
, we have

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\psi\circ\phi)=\mathrm{UMor}_{*}(\psi)\circ\mathrm{UMor}_{*}(\phi).

Note that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\phi)
is a
ring homomorphism
, as the superposition product
\mu
, making
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}
into an abelian topological monoid, is natural with respect to pointed maps
f\colon V_{k}\to V_{l}
.

Notation 3.12
.

We denote by
(-)_{x}\colon H_{k}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}x_{i}
the isomorphism of abelian groups with
[\gamma_{i}]\mapsto x_{i}
, and use the same notation for the map induced between exterior powers of
H_{k}
and
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}x_{i}
. Similarly
(-)_{y}\colon H_{k}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}y_{i}
is the isomorphism such that
[\gamma_{i}]\mapsto y_{i}
.

Theorem 3.13
.

For a group homomorphism
\phi\colon G_{k}\to G_{l}
,
the induced ring homomorphism
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\phi):\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)\to\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(l)
is given on the ring generators from Proposition
3.9
by

(1)

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\phi(\gamma_{i})\right]_{x}

(2)

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\phi(\gamma_{i})\right]_{y}+\left[c_{2}(\phi(\gamma_{i}))\right]_{x}.

Proof.

We observe that there is a natural inclusion
V_{k}\cong\widetilde{C}_{1}(V_{k})^{\infty}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{k})^{\infty}
, inducing on first homology the map
(-)_{x}\colon H_{k}\to\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)
with
[\gamma_{i}]\mapsto x_{i}
. This justifies (
1
).
We prove (
2
) in four steps, dealing with different types of maps
\phi
.

Step 1: Simple maps

Given a function
g:\{1,\dots,k\}\to\{0\}\sqcup\{1,\dots,l\}
, consider the group homomorphism
\phi_{g}:G_{k}\to G_{l}
with
\gamma_{i}\mapsto\gamma_{g(i)}
if
g(i)\geq 1
and
\gamma_{i}\mapsto 1
if
g(i)=0
. We can realise
\phi_{g}
by the based map of bouquets
f_{g}:V_{k}\to V_{l}
with
*\mapsto*
and

f_{g}:(t,i)\in(0,1)\times\{i\}\subset V_{k}\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(t,g(i))&\text{if }g(i)\geq 1,\\
&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We observe that
\widetilde{C}_{n}(f_{g})^{\infty}
maps the cell
e(i,n)
of
\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{k})^{\infty}
to the cell
e(g(i),n)
of
\widetilde{C}_{n}(V_{l})^{\infty}
via an oriented homeomorphism, and collapses it to the
0
-cell
\infty
otherwise. In particular,
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f_{g})
sends

y_{i}\mapsto y_{g(i)}
if
g(i)\geq 1
, and it sends

y_{i}\mapsto 0
otherwise.
This agrees with assertion (
2
), since
c_{2}(\gamma_{i})=0
.

Step 2: Pinch map

The pinch map
P:V_{1}\to V_{2}
, given by

P(t,1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(2t,1)&\text{if }0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
(2t-1,2)&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq 1,\end{array}\right.

realises the group homomorphism
p:G_{1}\to G_{2}
with
\gamma_{1}\mapsto\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}
. According to (
2
), we must show that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(P)
sends

y_{1}\mapsto y_{1}+y_{2}+x_{1}\wedge x_{2}
. We compute
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(P)(y_{1})
. The composition of the characteristic map
\Phi^{(2)}
relative to the cell
e(1,2)\subset\widetilde{C}_{2}(V_{1})^{\infty}
with the map
\widetilde{C}_{2}(P)^{\infty}
is given explicitly as the map
\widetilde{C}_{2}(P)^{\infty}\circ\Phi^{(2)}:\Delta^{2}\to\widetilde{C}_{2}(V_{2})^{\infty}
with

(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\left[(2t_{1},1),(2t_{2},1)\right]&\text{if }t_{1},t_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2},\\[2.84544pt]
\left[(2t_{1},1),(2t_{2}-1,2)\right]&\text{if }t_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\leq t_{2},\\[2.84544pt]
\left[(2t_{1}-1,2),(2t_{2}-1,2)\right]&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}\leq t_{1},t_{2}.\end{array}\right.

These three cases partition the domain
\Delta^{2}
into three regions
A^{(2,0)}
,
A^{(1,1)}
,
A^{(0,2)}
which are parametrised by
\Delta^{2}
,
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
,
\Delta^{2}
, respectively, via the oriented affine transformations
a_{(2,0)}:(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(2t_{1},2t_{2})
,
a_{(1,1)}:(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(2t_{1},2t_{2}-1)
,
a_{(0,2)}:(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(2t_{1},2t_{2}-1)
, as in Figure
1
. For each
\mathfrak{v}\in\{(2,0),(1,1),(0,2)\}
, we have an equality of maps

\Phi^{\mathfrak{v}}\circ a_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}=\widetilde{C}_{2}(P)^{\infty}\circ\Phi^{(2)}:A^{\mathfrak{v}}\to\widetilde{C}_{2}(V_{2})^{\infty}.

We conclude that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(P):y_{1}=e_{(2)}\mapsto e_{(2,0)}+e_{(0,2)}+e_{(1,1)}=y_{1}+y_{2}+x_{1}\wedge x_{2}
.

\Delta^{2}
t_{1}=t_{2}=1
t_{1}=t_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
t_{1}=0,\ t_{2}=1
t_{1}=\frac{1}{2},\ t_{2}=1
t_{1}=0,\ t_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
A^{(0,2)}
A^{(2,0)}
A^{(1,1)}

V_{2}

Figure 1.
The 2-simplex
\Delta^{2}
(above) maps under
\widetilde{C}_{2}(P)^{\infty}\circ\Phi^{(2)}
to configurations of
2
points in
V_{2}
. Region
A^{(2,0)}
corresponds (below) to configurations of two points on the first loop; region
A^{(1,1)}
to configurations of one point on each loop; region
A^{(0,2)}
to configurations of two points on the second loop.

Step 3: Maps from
G_{1}

For
w\in G_{l}
, let
\psi_{w}:G_{1}\to G_{l}
be the group homomorphism with
\gamma_{1}\mapsto w\in G_{l}
. Given
w,u\in G_{l}
, the homomorphism
\psi_{wu}
factors through

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}\cong G_{1}*G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{l}*G_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{w}*\psi_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{g}}

where
g:\{1,\dots,l,1^{\prime},\dots,l^{\prime}\}\to\{0,1,\dots,l\}
sends
i,i^{\prime}\mapsto i
.

We can now apply
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}
to composition (
3
), and evaluate at
y_{1}
leveraging Steps 1 and 2, to obtain

(4)

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\psi_{wu})(y_{1})=\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\psi_{w})(y_{1})+\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\psi_{u})(y_{1})+[w]_{x}\wedge[u]_{x}.

By decomposing
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\psi_{w})(y_{2})\in\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(l)
as the sum of components
Y(w)\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}y_{i}
and
C(w)\in\Lambda^{2}\mathopen{}\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}x_{i}\right)\mathclose{}
, equality (
4
) gives

(5)

\begin{split}Y(wu)&=Y(w)+Y(u);\\
C(wu)&=C(w)+C(u)+[w]_{x}\wedge[u]_{x}.\end{split}

Thus
Y:G_{l}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}y_{i}
is a group homomorphism, and it coincides with
[-]_{y}
on the generators
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{l}
by Step 1, and so
Y(-)=[-]_{y}
. Finally,
C
vanishes on words of length
1
by Step 1 and satisfies (
5
), so
C=c_{2}
by Lemma
3.11
.

Step 4: Any map
\phi:G_{k}\to G_{l}

If
\phi\colon G_{k}\to G_{l}
is any group homomorphism, by Step 1 we have that the generator
y_{i}\in\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(k)
is equal to the image of
y_{1}\in\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(1)
along the map
\phi_{g_{i}}\colon G_{1}\to G_{k}
, where
g_{i}:\{1\}\to\{0,...,k\}
takes
1
to
i
; we therefore compute

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\phi)(y_{i})=\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\phi\circ\phi_{g_{i}})(y_{1})

which, by Step 3, agrees with assertion (
2
) .
∎

4.
Cellular chains for configurations of surfaces

The setup of this section is analogous to
[
2
,
3
]
.

4.1.
A model for the surface
\Sigma_{g,1}

Let
g\geq 0
be a fixed integer throughout the section.
In the rectangle
\mathbf{R}=[0,2]\times[0,1]
, we decompose the side
\{2\}\times[0,1]
into
4g
consecutive closed intervals of equal length called
J_{1},\dots,J_{4g}
ordered and oriented with increasing second coordinate. Denote by
\tilde{p}_{0}\subset\{2\}\times[0,1]
the set of their endpoints.

Definition 4.1
.

We let
\mathcal{M}
be the quotient of
\mathbf{R}
obtained by identifying
J_{4i+1}
and
J_{4i+2}
with
J_{4i+3}
and
J_{4i+4}
, respectively, via their unique
orientation-reversing
isometry, for all
0\leq i\leq g-1
. We denote by
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathbf{R}\to\mathcal{M}
the quotient map. See Figure
2
.

The space
\mathcal{M}
is homeomorphic to
\Sigma_{g,1}
and admits a cell decomposition consisting of the following open cells:

•

one
0
-cell
p_{0}=\mathfrak{p}(\tilde{p}_{0})
;

•

(2g+1)
-many
1
-cells attached to
p_{0}
:

–

the images
I_{2i+j}=\mathfrak{p}(\mathring{J}_{4i+j})
, for
0\leq i\leq g-1
and
j=1,2
;

–

the image
I=\mathfrak{p}(\partial\mathbf{R}-\{2\}\times[0,1])
, coinciding with
\partial\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathopen{}\left\{p_{0}\right\}\mathclose{}
;

•

one
2
-cell, the image
\mathfrak{p}(\mathring{\mathbf{R}})
.

\end{overpic}

Figure 2.
The cell decomposition of
\mathcal{M}
in the case
g=2
.

Definition 4.2
.

We denote by
\iota:(V_{2g},*)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{M},p_{0})
the embedding induced by the map
\coprod_{i=1}^{2g}[0,1]\times\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{R}
sending
[0,1]\times\mathopen{}\left\{2j+\epsilon\right\}\mathclose{}
onto
J_{4j+\epsilon}
via the unique linear and orientation-preserving homeomorphism, for
0\leq j\leq g-1
and
\epsilon=1,2
.

The map
\iota
identifies the
i
th
circle of
V_{2g}
with the closure of
I_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq 2g
. In particular,
\iota
is a homeomorphism between
V_{2g}
and
\mathfrak{p}(\mathopen{}\left\{2\right\}\mathclose{}\times[0,1])\subset\mathcal{M}
.

4.2.
Cellular decomposition of
{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}

Recall from Notation
2.2
the shorthand
{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}:={C}_{n}(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
.

Definition 4.3
.

For
n\geq 0
, an
n
-
record
, denoted generically
\mathfrak{t}
, is a choice of the following set of data:

•

an integer
b\geq 0
;

•

a sequence
\underline{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{b})
of positive integers
P_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
;

•

a sequence
\mathfrak{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{2g})
of non-negative integers
v_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

satisfying the equality
P_{1}+\dots+P_{b}+v_{1}+\dots+v_{2g}=n
. We generically write
\mathfrak{t}=(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
,
n(\mathfrak{t}):=n
and
b(\mathfrak{t}):=b
. The
dimension
of
\mathfrak{t}
is
d(\mathfrak{t}):=n+b
. When
g=0
, we usually write a record as
(b,\underline{P})
.

\begin{overpic}[cell.pdf]
\put(58.0,25.0){$\mathbf{R}$} \put(94.0,3.0){$I_{1}$} \put(94.0,9.0){$I_{2}$} \put(91.75,15.0){$-I_{1}$} \put(91.75,21.0){$-I_{2}$} \put(94.0,27.0){$I_{3}$} \put(94.0,33.0){$I_{4}$} \put(91.75,39.0){$-I_{3}$} \put(91.75,45.0){$-I_{4}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3.
A configuration lying in the open cell
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\subset C_{12}(\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathfrak{t}=(4,(3,1,2,2),(1,2,0,1))
. Observe that on the right side, each point is drawn twice.

For each
n
-record
\mathfrak{t}
, let
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
be the subspace of configurations
s\in C_{n}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})\subset{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

for all
1\leq i\leq 2g
, exactly
v_{i}
points of the configuration
s
lie on
I_{i}
;

(2)

there are exactly
b
real numbers
0<x_{1}<\dots<x_{b}<2
such that
s
admits at least one point lying in
\mathring{\mathbf{R}}\cong\mathfrak{p}(\mathring{\mathbf{R}})\subset\mathring{\mathcal{M}}
having
x_{i}
as first coordinate;

(3)

for all
1\leq i\leq b
, exactly
P_{i}
points of
s\cap\mathring{\mathbf{R}}
have first coordinate equal to
x_{i}
. In other words,
s\cap\mathring{\mathbf{R}}
consists of
b
vertical “bars” with
P_{i}
points lying on the
i
th
bar from the left.

Each
s\in C_{n}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})\subset{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
lies in a unique subspace
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
. For a given
n
-record
\mathfrak{t}
, the space
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
is homeomorphic to an open disc of dimension
d(\mathfrak{t})
. Specifically, consider the polysimplex

\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}:=\Delta^{b}\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\Delta^{P_{i}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{2g}\Delta^{v_{i}}

and the map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}:\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}\to{C}_{d(\mathfrak{t})}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
sending the sequence of simplicial coordinates

\big((x_{1},\dots,x_{b}),(s^{(1)}_{1},\dots,s^{(1)}_{P_{1}}),\dots,(s^{(b)}_{1},\dots,s^{(b)}_{P_{b}}),(t_{1}^{(1)},\dots,t_{v_{1}}^{(1)}),\dots,(t_{1}^{(2g)},\dots,t_{v_{2g}}^{(2g)})\big)

to the following configuration, where
\cdot
denotes the superposition product:

\left[\mathfrak{p}(2x_{i},s^{(i)}_{j})\,|\,1\leq i\leq b,1\leq j\leq P_{i}\right]\cdot\left[\iota((t_{j}^{(i)},i))\,|\,1\leq i\leq 2g,1\leq j\leq v_{i}\right].

Each map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
restricts to a homeomorphism
\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}}\to e_{\mathfrak{t}}
, and maps the boundary
\partial\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
to the union of
\mathopen{}\left\{\infty\right\}\mathclose{}
and the subspaces
e^{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}
for varying
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
among the
n
-records satisfying
d(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})<d(\mathfrak{t})
. This leads to the following, which we state as a proposition for future reference.

Proposition 4.4
.

The space
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
admits a cell decomposition with a
0
-cell
\infty
, and a
d(\mathfrak{t})
-cell
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
, for each
n
-record
\mathfrak{t}
,
n\geq 0
. The orientations of the cells and the attaching maps are
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
.

4.3.
Cellular chain ring

In this subsection we will put a bigraded differential ring structure on the reduced cellular chain complex

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{t}}\mathbb{Z}e_{\mathfrak{t}}

(with the differential on the right hand side of the isomorphism determined below.) The most natural choice of a bigrading would put the generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
in weight
n(\mathfrak{t})
and homological degree
d(\mathfrak{t})
; we will use the following alternative, which will become convenient in the proof of Theorem
B
later.

Notation 4.5
.

We introduce a new grading with values in
\mathbb{Z}
, called the
bar-degree
and denoted
\star
. It is the difference between the
homological degree
, denoted
*
, and the
weight
, denoted
\bullet
. A bigraded abelian group will usually be endowed with the two gradings
(\bullet,\star)
; the
homological degree
, denoted
*
, can be recovered as
*:=\bullet+\star
.

For a record
\mathfrak{t}
, we put the generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
in weight
\bullet=-n(\mathfrak{t})
and in homological degree
*=b(\mathfrak{t})-n(\mathfrak{t})
; as a result, the bar-degree
\star
of
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
will be

*-\bullet=b(\mathfrak{t})-n(\mathfrak{t})-(-n(\mathfrak{t}))=b(\mathfrak{t}).

We justify this convention in a way similar to what we did in the discussion after Definition
3.4
: the generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
will contribute to the chain group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{d(\mathfrak{t})}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n(\mathfrak{t})}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
, and hence to the homology group
\widetilde{H}_{d(\mathfrak{t})}({C}_{n(\mathfrak{t})}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty})
, which is isomorphic to the
cohomology group

H^{2n(\mathfrak{t})-d(\mathfrak{t})}(C_{n(\mathfrak{t})}(\mathcal{M}))
; regarding cohomology as the bigraded dual of homology, and willing to consider
H_{i}(C_{n}(\mathcal{M}))
as living in homological degree
*=i
and in weight
\bullet=n
, we are thus led to put
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
in weight
-n(\mathfrak{t})
and in homological degree
-(2n(\mathfrak{t})-d(\mathfrak{t}))=b(\mathfrak{t})-n(\mathfrak{t})
.
Note that the differential
\partial
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
has bidegree
(\bullet,\star)=(0,-1)
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The superposition product
\mu\colon{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\times{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\to{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
is a cellular map, and thus it makes
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
into a commutative differential bigraded ring. Graded commutativity and the Leibniz rule hold with respect to the homological degree
*=\bullet+\star
.
In particular, for records
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, we have
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}=(-1)^{d(\mathfrak{t})d(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})}e_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Furthermore, for every record
(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
we have a factorisation
e_{(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})}=e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\cdot e_{(0,0,\mathfrak{v})}
.

Proof.

For a configuration
s\in{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\setminus\mathopen{}\left\{\infty\right\}\mathclose{}
, denote by
n(s)
,
d(s)
and
b(s)
the values
n(\mathfrak{t})
,
d(\mathfrak{t})
and
b(\mathfrak{t})
, respectively, where
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
is the unique cell containing
s
in its interior, and we use the notation from Definition
4.3
; we also set
d(\infty)=0
, as
\infty
is a 0-cell in
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
. To show that
\mu
is cellular with the product cellular structure on
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\times{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
it suffices that

d(\mu(s,s^{\prime}))\leq d(s)+d(s^{\prime})\ \mbox{ for all }\ s,s^{\prime}\in{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}.

If
\mu(s,s^{\prime})=\infty
, this is obvious, as we have
d(\mu(s,s^{\prime}))=0
. Otherwise both
s,s^{\prime}\neq\infty
, we have the inequality
b(\mu(s,s^{\prime}))\leq b(s)+b(s^{\prime})
and the equality
n(\mu(s,s^{\prime}))=n(s)+n(s^{\prime})
, and by using
d(s)=b(s)+n(s)
we are done.

The graded Leibniz rule for the differential
\partial
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
holds with respect to
d(\mathfrak{t})
, and similarly the commutativity of
\mu
implies that the induced product on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
satisfies
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}=(-1)^{d(\mathfrak{t})d(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})}e_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\cdot e_{\mathfrak{t}}
. But the homological degree
*=b(\mathfrak{t})-n(\mathfrak{t})
from Notation
4.5
and
d(\mathfrak{t})=b(\mathfrak{t})+n(\mathfrak{t})
have the same parity. Therefore the Leibniz rule and graded commutativity also hold with respect to
*
.

Finally, for a given record
(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
we have a canonical identification

\Delta^{(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})}=\Delta^{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\times\Delta^{(0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v})},

and the characteristic map
\Phi^{(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})}\colon\Delta^{(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})}\to{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
coincides with the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\times\Delta^{(0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\times{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\times\Phi^{(0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

proving the equality
e_{(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})}=e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\cdot e_{(0,0,\mathfrak{v})}
.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
n\geq 0
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\underline{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
N=\sum(1+P_{i}P_{j})
, where the sum is extended over all pairs of indices
1\leq i<j\leq n
satisfying
\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)
. The
\underline{P}
-twisted sign
of
\sigma
, denoted
\mathrm{sgn}_{\underline{P}}(\sigma)
, is
(-1)^{N}\in\mathopen{}\left\{\pm 1\right\}\mathclose{}
. (Note that if
2
divides all
P_{i}
, then
\mathrm{sgn}_{\underline{P}}(\sigma)=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)
.)

The following is immediate.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})
and
(b,\underline{P}^{\prime},\underline{0})
be records with vanishing third vector. Write
\underline{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{b})
and
\underline{P}^{\prime}=(P_{b+1},\dots,P_{b+b^{\prime}})
; denote by

\underline{P}*\underline{P}^{\prime}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{b+b^{\prime}})

the concatenation of
\underline{P}
and
\underline{P}^{\prime}
, and for a shuffle
\sigma
of type
(b,b^{\prime})
(see Definition
3.5
) let
\underline{P}*_{\sigma}\underline{P}^{\prime}=(P_{\sigma(1)},\dots,P_{\sigma(b+b^{\prime})})
.
Then we have

e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}\cdot e_{(b^{\prime},\underline{P}^{\prime},\underline{0})}=(-1)^{b^{\prime}(P_{1}+\dots+P_{b})}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}_{\underline{P}*\underline{P}^{\prime}}(\sigma)e_{(b+b^{\prime},\underline{P}*_{\sigma}\underline{P}^{\prime},\underline{0})}

where the sum is taken over all shuffles
\sigma
of type
(b,b^{\prime})
.

Recall now the embedding
\iota
from Definition
4.2
. The induced map

{C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}:\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{2g})^{\infty}\to{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}

is cellular, and in fact it exhibits
\widetilde{C}_{\bullet}(V_{2g})^{\infty}
as a subcomplex of
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
, spanned by
\infty
and all cells
e_{0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v}}
. Moreover, since superposition of configurations is natural in the space, we have that
{C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}
is a map of commutative monoids. Passing to reduced cellular chain complexes, the map
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}:\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{t}}\mathbb{Z}e_{\mathfrak{t}}
is the inclusion of differential algebras given by
e_{\mathfrak{v}}\mapsto e_{(0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v})}
. Notice that the inclusion
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
respects the weight
\bullet
(which by our conventions attains non-positive values on both sides), and if we put
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
in bar-degree
\star=0
, then

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
also respects the bar-degree. Combining this and Proposition
3.3
, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 4.9
.

There is a tensor product decomposition of bigraded rings

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)

where
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
is generated as a free abelian group by the cells of the form
e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}
.

Both factors are subrings of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
: the first by Lemma
4.8
, the second since it is the image of the ring homomorphism induced by
{C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}
. Only
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
, on which the differential is trivial, is a differential subring.

Remark 4.10
.

We used the superscript
B
for the subring
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
because we think of it as bar resolution for the homology of configurations on a disc. In fact, the factor
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
is responsible for the bar-degree of the elements in

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
.

4.4.
The differential on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}

Since
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
is a differential bigraded ring, its differential
\partial
is uniquely determined via the graded Leibniz rule by its behaviour on a set of ring generators, such as the elements
e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}
and
e_{(0,\underline{0},\mathfrak{v})}
for varying
(b,\underline{P})
and
\mathfrak{v}
; this uses Proposition
4.6
. On generators of the second kind the differential
\partial
vanishes, so we will focus on computing
\partial(e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})})
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
\omega
denote the element

\omega:=x_{1}\wedge x_{2}+x_{3}\wedge x_{4}+\dots+x_{2g-1}\wedge x_{2g}\in\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g),

where we use the notation from Proposition
3.9
.
For
k\geq 1
define
\Omega_{2k-1}:=0
and define
\Omega_{2k}\in\Lambda^{2k}_{\mathbb{Z}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2k}(2g)
as the element

\Omega_{2k}:=\frac{(2\omega)^{k}}{k!}=2^{k}\sum_{0\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq g-1}x_{2i_{1}+1}\wedge x_{2i_{1}+2}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{2i_{k}+1}\wedge x_{2i_{k}+2}.

Definition 4.12
.

We denote by
\zeta_{g}\in\pi_{1}(V_{2g},*)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{*2g}
the element

\zeta_{g}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\dots\gamma_{2g-1}\gamma_{2g}\gamma_{2g-1}^{-1}\gamma_{2g}^{-1}.

Lemma 4.13
.

Recall Definition
3.10
. Then
c_{2}(\zeta_{g})=2\omega\in\Lambda^{2}H_{2g}
.

Proof.

By property (2) from Lemma
3.11
, and using that

[\gamma_{2i}\gamma_{2i+1}\gamma_{2i}^{-1}\gamma_{2i+1}^{-1}]=0\in H_{2g}\ \mbox{ for all }\ 0\leq i\leq g-1,

we have
c_{2}(\zeta_{g})=\sum_{i=0}^{g-1}c_{2}(\gamma_{2i}\gamma_{2i+1}\gamma_{2i}^{-1}\gamma_{2i+1}^{-1})
.
Since
c_{2}
vanishes on each generator
\gamma_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}^{-1}
, applying property (2) we compute
c_{2}(\gamma_{2i}\gamma_{2i+1}\gamma_{2i}^{-1}\gamma_{2i+1}^{-1})=2[\gamma_{2i}]\wedge[\gamma_{2i+1}]
.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

The differential
\partial
restricted to
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}^{B}(\mathcal{M})
is the sum of two components

\partial_{B}+\partial_{M}

given on a generator
e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})}
by

\begin{split}\partial_{B}(e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})})&=\sum_{i=1}^{b-1}(-1)^{i}\mathbf{ss}(P_{i},P_{i+1})\ e_{(b-1,(P_{1},\dots,P_{i-1},P_{i}+P_{i+1},P_{i+2},\dots,P_{b}),\underline{0})};\\
\partial_{M}(e_{(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})})&=(-1)^{b}e_{(b-1,(P_{1},\dots,P_{b-1})),\underline{0})}\cdot\Omega_{P_{b}}.\end{split}

Proof.

Fix a record
\mathfrak{t}=(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})
, and endow the polysimplex

\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}=\Delta^{b}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b}}

with the product cell structure of the simplicial cell structures on the factors.

We claim that the map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}\colon\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}\to{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
is cellular, i.e. each open
k
-cell of
\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
is sent along
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
inside the
k
-skeleton of
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
; we will prove this claim by induction on
b\geq 0
. For the top cell this is tautological, as
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
is a characteristic map of a cell. We write
\partial\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
as a union of subcomplexes

\begin{array}[]{cl}\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}:=\partial_{i}\Delta^{b}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b}},&\mbox{for }0\leq j\leq b,\\
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{ver},i}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}:=\Delta^{b}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\partial_{j}\Delta^{P_{i}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b}},&\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq b\mbox{ and }0\leq j\leq P_{b},\end{array}

and check that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
is cellular on each of these subcomplexes.

The map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
restricts to the constant map to
\infty
on each subcomplex
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{ver},i}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
. Geometrically, this corresponds to the situation in which
either two points of the
i
th
bar collide (for
1\leq j\leq P_{i}-1
), or one point of the
i
th
bar goes to
\partial M
(for
j=0,P_{i}
). For
b=0
, this concludes the proof that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
is cellular.

We now assume
b\geq 1
and analyse the restriction of
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
on the subcomplexes
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
.
For
0\leq j\leq b
let
\iota_{j}\colon\Delta^{b-1}\to\Delta^{b}
be the standard inclusion of a face,
and denote by
\hat{\iota}_{j}\colon\Delta^{b-1}\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\Delta^{P_{i}}\to\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
the product map of
\iota_{j}
with the identity of
\prod_{i=1}^{b}\Delta^{P_{i}}
. Notice that
\hat{\iota}_{j}
is a cellular isomorphism between
\Delta^{b-1}\times\prod_{i=1}^{b}\Delta^{P_{i}}
and
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
.

The restriction of
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
on
\partial_{0}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
is again the constant map to
\infty
. Geometrically, this corresponds to the first bar going all the way to left and colliding onto
\partial\mathcal{M}
.

For
1\leq j\leq b-1
, the restriction of
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
on
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
corresponds geometrically to letting the
j
th
and
(j+1)
st
bars collide;
the map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\hat{\iota}_{j}
is equal to the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{b-1}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{j}}\times\Delta^{P_{j+1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{b-1}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{j}+P_{j+1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{P_{j},P_{j+1}}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{(b-1,\partial_{j}\underline{P},0)}}

where
\partial_{j}\underline{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}+P_{j+1},P_{j+2},\dots,P_{b})
and
\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{P_{j},P_{j+1}}
is the map from Definition
3.7
.
Thus the restriction of
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
on
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
is cellular, as it is the composite of cellular maps: by inductive hypothesis we know that
\Phi^{(b-1,\partial_{j}\underline{P},0)}
is cellular.

Finally, we have to treat the case
j=b
, which corresponds geometrically to the right-most bar colliding with
\iota(V_{2g})
.
Consider the element
\zeta_{g}\in\pi_{1}(V_{2g},*)=\mathbb{Z}^{*2g}
from Definition
4.12
,
and let
f_{\zeta_{g}}:(V_{1},*)\to(V_{2g},*)
be the based map realising the group homomorphism
\gamma_{1}\mapsto\zeta_{g}
“with constant speed”: concretely, recalling that
V_{1}=[0,1]/\mathopen{}\left\{0,1\right\}\mathclose{}
and that
\mathcal{M}
is a quotient of the rectangle
\mathbf{R}=[0,2]\times[0,1]
, then the map
[0,1]\to\mathbf{R}
with
t\mapsto(2,t)
descends to a map
f_{\zeta_{g}}\colon V_{1}\to\mathcal{M}
whose image is contained in the subspace
\iota(V_{2g})\subset\mathcal{M}
.
Then
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\hat{\iota}_{b}
is equal to the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{b-1}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b-1}}\times\Delta^{P_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{b-1}\times\Delta^{P_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{P_{b-1}}\times\widetilde{C}_{P_{b}}(V_{1})^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\times{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times\Phi_{P_{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{(b-1,\partial_{b}\underline{P},0)}\times{C}_{P_{b}}(f_{\zeta_{g}})^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where we use the following:

•

the characteristic map
\Phi_{P_{b}}\colon\Delta^{P_{b-1}}\to\widetilde{C}_{P_{b}}(V_{1})^{\infty}
in the cell decomposition of
\widetilde{C}_{P_{b}}(V_{1})^{\infty}
corresponds to the vector
\mathfrak{v}=(P_{b})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{1}
, i.e. to the cell
e(1,P_{b})
, as in Subsection
3.1
;

•

\partial_{b}\underline{P}
denotes the sequence
(P_{1},\dots,P_{b-1})
;

•

the map

{C}_{P_{b}}(f_{\zeta_{g}})^{\infty}\colon\widetilde{C}_{P_{b}}(V_{1})^{\infty}={C}_{P_{b}}(V_{1},*)^{\infty}\to{C}_{P_{b}}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}={C}_{P_{b}}(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})^{\infty}

is induced by the pointed map
f_{\zeta_{g}}
, and we include
{C}_{P_{b}}(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
inside
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
;

In particular
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}\circ\hat{\iota}_{b}
is exhibited as a composition of cellular maps, again using induction on
b
. This concludes the proof that
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}
is cellular.

We can in fact use the previous analysis to compute the differential
\partial e_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}^{B}(\mathcal{M})
as a sum of contributions coming from the different faces of
\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
. All faces
\partial^{\mathrm{ver},i}_{j}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
and the face
\partial_{0}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
will contribute trivially. For
1\leq j\leq b-1
, the face
\partial_{j}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta
will contribute by a multiple of
e_{(b-1,\partial_{j}\underline{P},\underline{0})}
, with coefficient being
(-1)^{j}\cdot\deg(\underline{\mathrm{sort}}_{P_{j},P_{j+1}})
, which by Lemma
3.8
gives
(-1)^{j}\cdot\mathbf{ss}(P_{j},P_{j+1})
. These contributions give the component
\partial_{B}(e_{\mathfrak{t}})
.
Finally, the face
\partial_{b}^{\mathrm{hor}}\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}
contributes by the product

e_{(b-1,\partial_{b}\underline{P},\underline{0})}\cdot\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(e(1,P_{b}))\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{},

using the tensor product decomposition from Proposition
4.9
.

By Theorem
3.13
, if
P_{b}=2k+1
, then

\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(e(1,P_{b}))=\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(x\frac{y^{k}}{k!})=[\zeta_{g}]_{x}\frac{([c_{2}(\zeta_{g})]_{x})^{k}}{k!}=0,

since
\zeta_{g}
is a commutator and hence
[\zeta_{g}]_{x}=0
; similarly, if
P_{b}=2k
, then

\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(e(1,P_{b}))=\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(\frac{y^{k}}{k!})=\frac{([c_{2}(\zeta_{g})]_{x})^{k}}{k!}=\frac{(2\omega)^{k}}{k!};

in the second to last equality we use that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is torsion-free as an abelian group, and in the last equality we use Lemma
4.13
.
In both cases

\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})(e(1,P_{b}))=\Omega_{P_{b}}.

∎

4.5.
Cellular approximation of diffeomorphisms

We let
D:=[0,1]\times[0,1]\subset\mathcal{M}
and identify the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,1}
with
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\partial\mathcal{M}))
.

Definition 4.15
.

For
1\leq t\leq 2
we define the self-map
\theta_{t}
of the pair
(\mathcal{M},\partial M)
as the map with

\mathfrak{p}(x,y)\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathfrak{p}(tx,y)&\text{if }x\leq\frac{2}{t},\\[5.69046pt]
\mathfrak{p}(2,y)&\text{if }x\geq\frac{2}{t}.\end{array}\right.

For
t=2
, we also write
\theta=\theta_{2}
. See Figure
4
.

The maps
\theta_{t}
assemble into a homotopy of self-maps of the pair
(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})
starting from the identity and ending with
\theta
.

Definition 4.16
.

We say that a self-map
f
of the pair
(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})
is
vertical
if for all
0\leq x\leq 2
there is
0\leq x^{\prime}\leq 2
such that
f(\mathfrak{p}(\mathopen{}\left\{x\right\}\mathclose{}\times[0,1]))\subseteq\mathfrak{p}(\mathopen{}\left\{x^{\prime}\right\}\mathclose{}\times[0,1])
.

Each map
\theta_{t}
is vertical, and for any homeomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\partial\mathcal{M})
the composition
\theta\circ f
(which is no longer a homeomorphism) is vertical. Compositions of vertical maps are vertical.

\begin{overpic}[tau2.pdf]
\put(49.0,10.0){$\overset{\theta}{\to}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.
The map
\theta
expands
D
and contracts horizontally the rest of
\mathcal{M}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
f
be a vertical self-map of the pair
(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})
; then the induced map
{C}_{\bullet}(f)^{\infty}\colon{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\to{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
is cellular.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 0
be fixed; in the notation of the proof of Proposition
4.6
, it suffices to show the inequality
d({C}_{n}(f)^{\infty}(s))\leq d(s)
, for all
s\in{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
. If
{C}_{n}(f)^{\infty}(s)=\infty
, this holds immediately. Otherwise, we have
n({C}_{n}(f)^{\infty}(s))=n(s)=n
; moreover, since
f
is vertical, we have
b({C}_{n}(f)^{\infty}(s))\leq b(s)
. Using the equality
d(-)=n(-)+b(-)
, the conclusion follows.
∎

In particular every vertical map of
(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})
induces an endomorphism of the chain complex
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
. The homotopy of vertical maps
\theta_{t}
induces a homotopy of cellular self-maps of
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
, which in turn induces a
homotopy through self-chain maps
of the chain complex
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
; clearly the space of self-chain maps of a chain complex is discrete and, furthermore, the mapping degree for maps between spheres, the quantity which determines chain maps, is locally constant; we conclude that
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{M}}
induces
the same
self-chain map on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
as
\theta
; in other words,
\theta
acts as the identity on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
.
More generally, if
f\simeq g
are isotopic homeomorphisms in
\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\partial\mathcal{M})
, then
f\circ\theta
is homotopic to
g\circ\theta
through vertical maps, hence

{C}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f)^{\infty}
is homotopic to
C_{n}(\theta\circ g)
through cellular maps, and hence
\theta\circ f
and
\theta\circ g
induce the same self-chain map of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
.

Finally, if
f,g\in\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\partial\mathcal{M})
, then
\theta\circ f\circ\theta\circ g
is homotopic to
\theta\circ f\circ g
through vertical maps, so that again
\theta\circ f\circ\theta\circ g
and
\theta\circ f\circ g
induce the same
self-chain map of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
. This leads to the following proposition.

Proposition 4.18
.

The cellular chain complex
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
admits an action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
by self-chain maps, defined by letting the class of a homeomorphism
f\in\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\mathcal{M})
act by the chain map induced by the cellular map
{C}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f)^{\infty}
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Under the decomposition of Proposition
4.9
, the subrings

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})\ \mbox{ and }\ \mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)

of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
are
\Gamma_{g,1}
-subrepresentations. More precisely, the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
is trivial, and
f\in\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\mathcal{M})
acts on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
via

C_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f|_{\iota(V_{2g})})_{*}=\mathrm{UMor}(\theta\circ f|_{\iota(V_{2g})})=\mathrm{UMor}(\phi),

where
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{*2g})
is the automorphism of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1})\cong\pi_{1}(V_{2g},*)
induced by
f
.

Proof.

For any
f\in\mathrm{Homeo}(\mathcal{M},D\cup\partial\mathcal{M})
,
the self-map
{C}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f)^{\infty}
of
{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
is induced by a self-map of
(\mathcal{M},\partial\mathcal{M})
, so it respects the product
\mu
; it follows that
\mathrm{UMor}(\theta\circ f|_{\iota(V_{2g})})
is a ring automorphism.
Let now
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
be a generator in
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
, for some record
\mathfrak{t}
of the form
(b,\underline{P},\underline{0})
with
b\geq 1
. We want to study the behaviour of
{C}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f)^{\infty}
on the open cell
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\subseteq{C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
. Let
e^{1/2}_{\mathfrak{t}}
be the subspace of
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
containing configurations
s
which are supported in the left, open half of
\mathring{\mathbf{R}}
, i.e. for each point
\mathfrak{p}(x,y)\in s
we have
0<x<1
. Then
e^{1/2}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is open in
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
; moreover
\theta\circ f
maps
e^{1/2}_{\mathfrak{t}}
homeomorphically onto
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
, and maps the complement of
e^{1/2}_{\mathfrak{t}}
to configurations
s^{\prime}
satisfying
b(s^{\prime})<b(s)=b
. It follows that the generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
is fixed by the chain map
{C}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ f)^{\infty}_{*}
; this proves that
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}_{*}(\mathcal{M})
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
, and the restricted action is trivial.

The fact that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is also invariant under the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
, and that the restricted action coincides with the one described in Theorem
3.13
, follows from the fact that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is the image of the ring homomorphism
{C}_{\bullet}(\iota)^{\infty}_{*}
.
∎

This proposition combined with Proposition
2.3
reduces the problem of determining the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representation
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1}))
to studying the action of
\Gamma_{g,1}

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
. This is done in the next section.

5.
The
