\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on the homology of configurations

We fix a prime number
p
and
g\geq 0
throughout the section. We abbreviate
G=G_{2g}=\mathbb{Z}^{*2g}\cong\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1})
, with the isomororphism specified in Section
4
, and
H=G^{\mathrm{ab}}\cong H_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1})
.

\partial\Sigma_{g,1}
\Sigma_{g,1}
p_{0}
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{3}
\gamma_{4}
\dots
a
\gamma_{2g-1}
\gamma_{2g}

Figure 5.
The standard generators of
G=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1},p_{0})
and the curve
a
from the proof of Lemma
5.16
.

5.1.
Recollections on Torelli groups and Johnson homomorphisms

Definition 5.1
.

The
Torelli group
, denoted
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, is the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1};\mathbb{Z})
. The
Torelli group modulo
p
, denoted
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
, is the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
action on
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1};\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

Theorem 5.2

(Cooper
[
10
]
, Perron
[
24
]
)
.

For a prime
p
, the group
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
is generated by
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
and the
p
th
powers of all Dehn twists.

In
[
16
]
, Johnson proved that the quotient
[G,G]/[G,[G,G]]
of lower central subgroups of
G
is a free abelian group, isomorphic to
\Lambda^{2}H
as
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representation. The explicit isomorphism
j\colon[G,G]/[G,[G,G]]\to\Lambda^{2}H
is given by sending the class of
w_{1}w_{2}w_{1}^{-1}w_{2}^{-1}
in
[G,G]/[G,[G,G]]
to
[w_{1}]\wedge[w_{2}]\in\Lambda^{2}H
, for
w_{1},w_{2}\in G
.

Definition 5.3

(Johnson
[
16
]
)
.

The
Johnson homomorphism
is the group homomorphism

\tau_{g,1}:\mathcal{T}_{g,1}\to\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)

sending
\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
to the homomorphism of abelian groups
[w]\mapsto j([\phi(w)w^{-1}]_{[G,[G,G]]})
.

Johnson showed that
\tau_{g,1}
is a well defined group homomorphism, and computed its image using the identification
\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\cong H^{\vee}\otimes\Lambda^{2}H\cong H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H
, where the self duality
H\cong H^{\vee}
comes from the symplectic form on
H=H_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1})
.

Theorem 5.4

(Johnson
[
16
]
)
.

The image of
\tau_{g,1}
is the subgroup
\Lambda^{3}H\subset H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H
, where the inclusion is given by sending the triple wedge
a\wedge b\wedge c\in\Lambda^{3}H
to the sum

a\otimes b\wedge c+b\otimes c\wedge a+c\otimes a\wedge b\in H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H.

We observe that
H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H
is a free abelian group of rank
2g\binom{2g}{2}
, and
\Lambda^{3}H
is a split free abelian subgroup of rank
\binom{2g}{3}
.

The kernel of
\tau_{g,1}
is the so-called
Johnson kernel

\mathcal{K}_{g,1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
.

Theorem 5.5

(Johnson
[
16
]
)
.

If
g\geq 0
, the group
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
is generated by all Dehn twists around separating simple closed curves in
\Sigma_{g,1}
.

5.2.
The maps
\xi
and
\xi^{p}

Notation 5.6
.

Recall Proposition
3.9
and Theorem
3.13
; we identify the abelian group
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
with

\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g}\mathbb{Z}y_{i}\oplus\Lambda^{2}\mathopen{}\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g}\mathbb{Z}x_{i}\right)\mathclose{}\cong H\oplus\Lambda^{2}H.

We observe that
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
has a naïve
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action as the direct sum of the representations
H
and
\Lambda^{2}H
. In light of Proposition
4.19
, it also inherits an action via
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(\theta\circ-)
.
For both actions of
\Gamma_{g,1}
on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
we have a short exact sequence of
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representations

\Lambda^{2}H\to\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\to H,

and for the naïve action there is a
\Gamma_{g,1}
-equivariant splitting
H\to\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
.

Notation 5.7
.

For
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
denote the naïve action by
\phi*_{H}-
and the action via
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(\theta\circ-)
by
\phi*-
.

Definition 5.8
.

We define a function of sets
\xi:\Gamma_{g,1}\to\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)
by sending
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
to the homomorphism of abelian groups sending the basis element
[\gamma_{i}]\in H
to the element
c_{2}(\phi(\gamma_{i}))\in\Lambda^{2}H
.
Composing with the modulo
p
reduction, we similarly define
\xi^{p}:\Gamma_{g,1}\to\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

We let
\xi_{\tau}
(resp. by
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
) be the restriction of
\xi
(resp.
\xi^{p}
) on
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
(resp. on
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
).

The function
\xi
enjoys a special relationship with the Johnson homomorphism.

Proposition 5.9
.

\xi_{\tau}=2\tau_{g,1}
.

Proof.

This is contained in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 6.7]
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
a\in H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
and
b\in\Lambda^{2}H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
; then for
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
we have

\phi*(a\oplus b)=\phi*_{H}a\oplus(\phi*_{H}b+\xi(\phi)(a))
.

Proof.

Applying Theorem
3.13
proves the equality on the basis of
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
given by the elements
y_{i}
and
x_{j}\wedge x_{k}
, for
1\leq i,j,k\leq 2g
.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

For
\phi,\psi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
and
a\in H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
, we have

\xi(\phi\psi)(a)=\phi*_{H}\xi(\psi)(a)+\xi(\phi)(\psi*_{H}a).

Proof.

We leave the proof to the reader.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

The maps
\xi_{\tau}
and
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
are group homomorphisms.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, for any
\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, the map
\phi*_{H}-
is the identity map. Then, for
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, Proposition
5.11
yields

\xi_{\tau}(\phi\psi)(a)=\xi_{\tau}(\phi)(a)+\xi_{\tau}(\psi)(a)\ \mbox{ for all }\ a\in H.

Thus
\xi_{\tau}
is a group homomorphism.
The same argument modulo
p
works for
\xi_{\tau}^{p}
, using that the naïve action of
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
on
H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is trivial.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

It can be shown that
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
is equal to
2\tau^{Z}_{1}(p)
, where

\tau^{Z}_{1}(p)\colon\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)\to\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}

is the Johnson-Zassenhaus homomorphism from
[
10
,
29
]
. However the definition of
\tau^{Z}_{1}(p)
is a bit complicated, hence we prefer to avoid comparing
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
to
\tau^{Z}_{1}(p)
and directly prove the needed properties of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
.

The normal subgroup
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}\subset\Gamma_{g,1}
admits an action of
\Gamma_{g,1}
by conjugation, and the abelian group
\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)
has a
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action defined by

f\mapsto(\phi*_{H}-)\circ f\circ(\phi^{-1}*_{H}-)\ \mbox{ for }\ \phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}\ \mbox{ and }\ f\in\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H).

Similarly,
\Gamma_{g,1}
acts on
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
and
\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Under the above actions,
\xi_{\tau}
and
\xi_{\tau}^{p}
are
\Gamma_{g,1}
-equivariant.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
5.11
twice, for
a\in H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
and
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, we get a chain of equalities

\displaystyle\xi_{\tau}(\phi\psi\phi^{-1})(a)=(\phi\psi)*_{H}\xi_{\tau}(\phi^{-1})(a)+\xi_{\tau}(\phi\psi)(\phi^{-1}*_{H}a)

\displaystyle=\phi*_{H}\psi*_{H}\xi_{\tau}(\phi^{-1})(a)+\mathopen{}\left(\phi*_{H}\xi_{\tau}(\psi)(\phi^{-1}*_{H}a)+\xi_{\tau}(\phi)(\psi*_{H}\phi^{-1}*_{H}a)\right)\mathclose{}.

But, as
\psi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
, the sum of the first and third summand vanishes:

\phi*_{H}\xi_{\tau}(\phi^{-1})(a)+\xi_{\tau}(\phi)(\phi^{-1}*_{H}a)=\xi_{\tau}(\phi\phi^{-1})(a)=\xi_{\tau}(\mathrm{id})(a)=0.

Again, we used Proposition
5.11
. The conclusion follows. The argument for
\xi_{\tau}^{p}
is analogous, considering the above chains of equalities modulo
p
.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

The definition of the maps of sets
\xi
and
\xi^{p}
really depends on a choice of generators for the free group
G
; Lemma
5.14
however proves that the restrictions
\xi_{\tau}
and
\xi_{\tau}^{p}
are independent of such choice.

Recall from Theorem
5.5
that all separating Dehn twists lie in
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
, and from Theorem
5.2
that all
p
th
powers of Dehn twists lie in
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
.

Lemma 5.16
.

All
p
th
powers of non-separating Dehn twists are in the kernel of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
.
All separating Dehn twists are in the kernel of
\xi_{\tau}
.

Proof.

All non-separating Dehn twists in
\Gamma_{g,1}
are conjugate to each other, so it suffices to show that the
p
th
power of a specific Dehn twist is in the kernel of
\xi^{p}
.
Let
a
be a simple closed curve freely homotopic to
\gamma_{2g}
, as in Figure
5
, and let
D_{a}\in\Gamma_{g,1}
be the associated Dehn twist. Then
D_{a}(\gamma_{2g})=\gamma_{2g-1}\gamma_{2g}
, whereas
D_{a}(\gamma_{i})=\gamma_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq 2g-1
.
It follows that
D_{a}^{p}
sends
\gamma_{2g}\mapsto\gamma_{2g-1}^{p}\gamma_{2g}
and fixes the other generators
\gamma_{i}
; therefore the group homomorphism

\xi(D_{a}^{p})\colon H\to\Lambda^{2}H
sends

[\gamma_{2g}]\mapsto c_{2}(D_{a}^{p}(\gamma_{2g}))=\binom{p}{2}[\gamma_{2g-1}]\wedge[\gamma_{2g-1}]+p[\gamma_{2g-1}]\wedge[\gamma_{2g}]=p[\gamma_{2g-1}]\wedge[\gamma_{2g}]

and sends
\gamma_{i}\mapsto c_{2}(D_{a}^{p}(\gamma_{i}))=c_{2}(\gamma_{i})=0
for
i\neq 2g
.
Modulo
p
, we obtain the zero group homomorphism, i.e.
\xi^{p}(D^{p}_{a})=0
.

To prove that separating Dehn twists lie in the kernel of
\xi_{\tau}
, we use the equality
\xi_{\tau}=2\tau_{g,1}
together with the fact that by Theorem
5.5
the kernel of
\tau_{g,1}
is precisely the subgroup of
\Gamma_{g,1}
generated by all separating Dehn twists.
∎

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
p
be an odd prime. Then the image of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
is isomorphic to
\Lambda^{3}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\subset H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and the kernel of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
is generated by the kernel of
\xi_{\tau}
and
p
th
powers of Dehn twists.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.16
all
p
th
powers of Dehn twists are in the kernel of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
, and by Theorem
5.2
they generate
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
together with
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
; it follows that

\mathrm{Im}(\xi^{p}_{\tau})=\mathrm{Im}(\xi^{p}_{\tau}|_{\mathcal{T}_{g,1}})=\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{\tau}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}).

Using
\xi_{\tau}=2\tau_{g,1}
and the assumption that
p
is odd, we have that the maps

\xi_{\tau}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p},\tau_{g,1}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\colon\mathcal{T}_{g,1}\to\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}

have the same image and the same kernel.

Recall from Theorem
5.4
that
\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})\subset\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\cong H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H
is the split subgroup
\Lambda^{3}H
; thus we can identify
\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{\tau}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})=\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})\subset H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\cong\Lambda^{3}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
; this proves the first claim.

For the second claim, we first observe that
\ker(\xi_{\tau}^{p})
is generated by
p
th
powers of Dehn twists together with
\ker(\xi_{\tau}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})=\ker(\tau_{g,1}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})\subset\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
. From the first isomorphism theorem, there is a short sequence of groups

\ker(\tau_{g,1})\to\ker(\tau_{g,1}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})\to p\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\cap\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1});

the third term can be identified with
p\Lambda^{3}H=p\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})
, using again that the inclusion
\Lambda^{3}H\subset H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H
is split.
In particular
\ker(\tau_{g,1}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
can be generated by
\ker(\tau_{g,1})
and lifts in
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
of generators of the group
p\Lambda^{3}H
; for the latter we may take the
p
th
powers of a generating set of
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
. We now observe that
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
is generated by separating twists and
bounding pairs

D_{a}D_{a^{\prime}}^{-1}
, i.e. products of a Dehn twist
D_{a}
with the inverse of a Dehn twist
D_{a^{\prime}}
, where
a,a^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{g,1}
are disjoint simple closed curves cobounding a subsurface of
\Sigma_{g,1}
. We conclude that the following elements generate
\ker(\xi^{p}_{\tau})
:

(1)

all
p
th
powers of all Dehn twists;

(2)

all separating Dehn twists;

(3)

all
p
th
powers of bounding pairs.

We may drop the third kind of generators from the generating set: for a bounding pair
D_{a}D_{a^{\prime}}^{-1}
, we have that the Dehn twists
D_{a}
and
D_{a^{\prime}}
commute, hence
(D_{a}D_{a^{\prime}}^{-1})^{p}=D_{a}^{p}D_{a^{\prime}}^{-p}
, which is already an element in the subgroup of
\Gamma_{g,1}
generated by
p
th
powers of Dehn twists.
∎

Lemma
5.16
and
Proposition
5.17
justify the following definition.

Definition 5.18
.

For an odd prime
p
, the mod-
p
Johnson kernel is
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p):=\ker(\xi^{p}_{\tau})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
, i.e. the subgroup generated by separating Dehn twists and
p
th
powers of Dehn twists.

5.3.
The
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on integral homology

Proposition 5.19
.

The kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is the Johnson kernel
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
act trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\cong H\oplus\Lambda^{2}H
, where we use the splitting from Notation
5.6
. Then
\phi
acts trivially on the subrepresentation
\Lambda^{2}H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
and on the quotient
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\Lambda^{2}H\cong H
, so that
\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
.
Furthermore, by Proposition
5.10

\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
precisely if
\xi_{\tau}(\phi)=0
. Since
\xi_{\tau}=2\tau_{g,1}
and the target of
\xi_{\tau}
is torsion-free, it follows that
\tau_{g,1}(\phi)=0
as well, and thus
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
.

Conversely,
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
is generated by separating Dehn twists; hence
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-1}(2g)
, and combining Lemma
5.16
and Proposition
5.10
we obtain that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
also acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
. The action of
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is by ring automorphisms, and the torsion-free ring
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is generated, at least after tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
, by
\mathrm{UMor}_{-1}(2g)
and
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
. This proves that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, and hence on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
.
∎

The element
\Omega_{2}=2\omega\in\Lambda^{2}H\subset\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
from Definition
4.11
is
\Gamma_{g,1}
-invariant, so the quotient
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}
is also a
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representation.

Lemma 5.20
.

The kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes R/R\Omega_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
for
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

We assume
R=\mathbb{Z}
; the case
R=\mathbb{Q}
is identical. The fact that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}
is a consequence of Proposition
5.19
.
Conversely, if a class
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}
, then it also acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\Lambda^{2}H\cong H
, hence
\phi
must lie in
\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
. Furthermore,
\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}
if and only if
\xi_{\tau}(\phi)(a)\in\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}\subset\Lambda^{2}H
for all
a\in H
.
Since
\xi_{\tau}=2\tau_{g,1}
and
\Omega_{2}=2\omega
, and since multiplication by
2
is injective on
\Lambda^{2}H
, the condition on
\phi
can be rewritten as

\tau_{g,1}(\phi)\in\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{Z}\omega)\subset\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)
.
We will prove that

\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})\cap\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2})=0\subset\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H);

from this the assertion
\ker(\tau_{g,1})=\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
will follow.

We use the identification

\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\cong H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H

already considered in the discussion before Theorem
5.4
, and embed

\Lambda^{2}H\hookrightarrow H\otimes H

via
a\wedge b\mapsto a\otimes b-b\otimes a
; we thus obtain an embedding of
H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H\hookrightarrow H\otimes H\otimes H
.
Under this embedding,
\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{Z}\omega)

is the subgroup of
H^{\otimes 3}
consisting of the elements

a\otimes\omega:=\sum_{i=1}^{g}a\otimes x_{2i-1}\otimes x_{2i}-a\otimes x_{2i}\otimes x_{2i-1},

for varying
a\in H
, whereas
\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})\cong\Lambda^{3}H
is the subgroup of
H^{\otimes 3}
generated by all elements of the form

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)a_{\sigma(1)}\otimes a_{\sigma(2)}\otimes a_{\sigma(3)},

for varying
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in H
.
In particular, each element of
\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})
is invariant under the cyclic permutation of tensors
C\colon H^{\otimes 3}\to H^{\otimes 3}
acting as
a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes a_{3}\mapsto a_{2}\otimes a_{3}\otimes a_{1}
.

Let now
a\in H
be such that
a\otimes\omega\in\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{g,1})
, and write
a\otimes\omega
as a linear combination
\sum_{1\leq j,k,l\leq 2g}c_{j,k,l}x_{j}\otimes x_{k}\otimes x_{l}
of the basis elements
x_{j}\otimes x_{k}\otimes x_{l}
of
H^{\otimes 3}
, with
c_{j,k,l}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Using the definition of
a\otimes\omega
we get
c_{j,k,l}=0
for all
j,k,l
such that
\mathopen{}\left\{k,l\right\}\mathclose{}
is not a pair of the form
\mathopen{}\left\{2i+1,2i+2\right\}\mathclose{}
for
i\in\{0,1,...,g-1\}
; using invariance of
a\otimes\omega
under
C
, we obtain that
c_{j,k,l}=0
for every
j,k,l
such that at least one of the pairs
\mathopen{}\left\{j,k\right\}\mathclose{}
,
\mathopen{}\left\{k,l\right\}\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\{l,j\right\}\mathclose{}
is not a pair of the form
\mathopen{}\left\{2i+1,2i+2\right\}\mathclose{}
; this clearly implies
c_{j,k,l}=0
for all
j,k,l
, so that
a\otimes\omega=0
.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

For
n\geq 2
and
R=\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
H_{2n-2}({C}_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};R)
is the Johnson kernel
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
.

Proof.

Propositions
4.19
and
5.19
together imply that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on the cellular chains
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
, thus also on the homology
\widetilde{H}_{*}({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
for all
n\geq 0
.
To prove that the kernel is precisely
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
, it suffices by Lemma
5.20
to embed
\mathrm{UMor}(2)/\langle 2\omega\rangle
as a
\Gamma_{g,1}
-subrepresentation of
H_{2n-2}({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
.

For
l\geq 0
denote by
1^{l}
the
l
-record
(l,(1,\dots,1),\underline{0})

and by
1^{l}2
the
(l+2)
-record
(l,(1,\dots,1,2),\underline{0})
.
By Proposition
4.14
, the differential
\partial(e_{1^{l}})
vanishes for all
l\geq 0
; in particular the differential vanishes on the abelian groups

\begin{split}&\mathbb{Z}e_{1^{n-2}}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{n-2}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{},\\
&\mathbb{Z}e_{1^{n-1}}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{-1}(2g)\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{n-1}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}.\end{split}

The first vanishing implies that
\mathbb{Z}e_{\bar{1}}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
only consists of
cycles. The second vanishing, together with the direct sum decomposition

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{n-1}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\mathbb{Z}e_{1^{n-1}}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{-1}(2g)\ \oplus\ \mathbb{Z}e_{1^{n-2}2}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{0}(2g)

implies that the boundaries in
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{n-2}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
are generated by the image of the restriction of
the differential on
\mathbb{Z}e_{1^{n-2}2}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{0}(2g)
,
which is generated by the element
e_{1^{n-2}2}=e_{1^{n-2}2}\otimes 1
, and again by Proposition
4.14
we have

\partial(e_{1^{n-2}2})=e_{1^{n-2}}\otimes\Omega_{2}.

Thus
e_{1^{n-2}}
gives an injection of
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)/\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2}
into
\widetilde{H}_{2n-2}({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
, which is
\Gamma_{g,1}
-equivariant.

∎

5.4.
The
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-homology

Let
p
be an odd prime.
The homology
\widetilde{H}_{*}({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty};\mathbb{F}_{p})
is computed by the complex

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
with the induced differential and
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action. The arguments of this subsection are analogous to the ones of Subsection
5.3
, so we emphasise only the differences.

Proposition 5.22
.

The kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
.

Proof.

The first part of the proof of Proposition
5.19
can be adapted word by word modulo
p
to show that
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
if and only if
\phi\in\ker(\xi^{p}_{\tau})
: we use that the short exact sequence of
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representations
\Lambda^{2}H\to\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\to H
is split as a sequence of abelian groups, hence after tensoring with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
we obtain a short exact sequence
\Lambda^{2}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\to\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\to H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
. This shows that the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is contained in
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
.

By Proposition
3.9
,
the ring
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is generated (as a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra) by the elements
x_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq 2g
, and by the divided powers
y_{i}^{[m]}
, for
1\leq i\leq 2g
and
m\geq 1
; it follows that the elements
x_{i}\otimes 1
and
y_{i}^{[m]}\otimes 1
generate the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Therefore, to prove that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
, it suffices to show that for
\phi\in\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)=\ker(\xi^{p}_{\tau})
we have
\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)(y_{i}^{[m]})\equiv y_{i}^{[m]}\mod p
, i.e. the difference
\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)(y_{i}^{[m]})-y_{i}^{[m]}
is a multiple of
p
in the abelian group
\mathrm{UMor}_{2m}(2g)
. We know that
\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)(y_{i})\equiv y_{i}\mod p
, i.e. there is
a\in\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)
such that
\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)(y_{i})=y_{i}+pa
; for
m\geq 1
we then have

\displaystyle\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)\left(y_{i}^{[m]}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(y_{i}+pa)^{m}}{m!}\equiv y_{i}^{[m]}\mod p,

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\mathrm{UMor}(\phi)
is a ring homomorphism and
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
is torsion-free.
∎

Lemma 5.23
.

The kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-actions on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}\Omega_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
.

Proof.

The argument is the same as in the proof of Lemma
5.20
. By Proposition
5.22
we know that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
, hence on its quotient
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}\Omega_{2}
.

Conversely, if
\phi\in\Gamma_{g,1}
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}\Omega_{2}
then it also acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\Lambda^{2}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\cong H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
, hence
\phi\in\mathcal{T}_{g,1}(p)
; the condition that
\phi
acts trivially on
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}\Omega_{2}
can then be rephrased as

\xi^{p}_{\tau}(\phi)\in\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{Z}\Omega_{2})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}=\mathrm{Hom}(H,\mathbb{Z}\omega)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\subset\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p},

where in the last step we use that
p
is odd, and that
\mathbb{Z}\omega\subset\Lambda^{2}H
is a split subgroup.

We can now identify
\mathrm{Hom}(H,\Lambda^{2}H)\cong H\otimes\Lambda^{2}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
with a subgroup of
H^{\otimes 3}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
as in the proof of Lemma
5.20
; by Proposition
5.17
, the image of
\xi^{p}_{\tau}
can then be identified with
\Lambda^{3}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and all we have to prove is that the subgroups
\Lambda^{3}H\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
H\otimes\mathbb{Z}\omega\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
of
H^{\otimes 3}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
intersect trivially: this can be done by repeating the final argument used in the proof of Lemma
5.20
.
∎

Proposition 5.24
.

For
n\geq 2
, the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on the homology group
H_{2n-2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};\mathbb{F}_{p})
is the mod-
p
Johnson kernel
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.22
, the group
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
acts trivially on the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-equivariant chain complex
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty},\mathbb{F}_{p}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{B}({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty})\otimes(\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
, hence on its homology. To show that the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
H_{2n-2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};\mathbb{F}_{p})
is no larger than
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}(p)
, for a fixed
n\geq 2
, it suffices by Lemma
5.23
to embed a copy of
\mathrm{UMor}_{-2}(2g)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}/\mathbb{F}_{p}\Omega_{2}

inside
H_{2n-2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};\mathbb{F}_{p})
, and this can be done by the same argument used in the proof of Proposition
5.21
, tensoring with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A
.

Propositions
4.19
and
5.19
together imply that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on the cellular chains
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
which, after tensoring with
R
and dualising, computes
\widetilde{H}^{*}({C}_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};R)
. It follows that
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
acts trivially on this cohomology and, by Proposition
2.3
, the same holds for
H_{*}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
.

In particular, we have an isomorphism
\widetilde{H}_{2n-2}({C}_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1})^{\infty};\mathbb{Q})\cong H^{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
of
\Gamma_{g,1}
-representations. It follows by Proposition
5.21
that the kernel of the
\Gamma_{g,1}
-action on
H^{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
, and thus also on its linear dual
H_{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
, is
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
for
n\geq 2
. Since
H_{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})\cong H_{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, then
H_{2}(C_{n}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
has kernel at least
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
; by (1) the kernel is precisely
\mathcal{K}_{g,1}
, and (2) follows.

The case
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
follows analogously using Propositions
5.22
and
5.24
.
∎

Part II
Homology computation up to an Ext problem

In this part of the article we prove Theorem
B
and its corollaries. We fix
g\geq 0
throughout the part and denote by
\mathcal{M}
the model of
\Sigma_{g,1}
from Definition
4.1
.

6.
Proof of Theorem
B
and its corollaries

6.1.
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
as a bar complex

The starting point towards the proof of Theorem
B
is a reinterpretation of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
. Recall from Definition
4.12
the element
\zeta\in\pi_{1}(V_{2g},*)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{*2g}
, and the corresponding pointed map
f_{\zeta_{g}}\colon(V_{1},*)\to(V_{2g},*)
from the proof of Proposition
4.14
.
The ring homomorphism

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f_{\zeta_{g}})\colon\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)\to\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)

makes
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
into a weighted
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)
-left module. Following Definition
3.4
, observe that
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
and
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)
are concentrated in non-positive weights.

Recall from Proposition
3.9
the isomorphisms
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}(x)\otimes\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)
and

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})\otimes\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g})
. By Theorem
3.13
we have that

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f_{\zeta_{g}})
sends
x\mapsto 0
and
y^{[m]}\mapsto\Omega_{2m}
, for
m\geq 1
(see Definition
4.11
).
We regard
\mathbb{Z}
as a
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)
-
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)
-bimodule concentrated in weight
0
, via the ring homomorphism
\varepsilon\colon\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)\to\mathbb{Z}
sending
x,y^{[m]}\mapsto 0
.
We can therefore consider the reduced bar complex

\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)),

i.e. the weighted chain complex having
\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)_{+}^{\otimes b}\otimes\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)
in bar-degree
\star=b\geq 0
, and vanishing in negative bar-degrees. Here
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)_{+}:=\ker(\varepsilon)
denotes the augmentation ideal.
For
b\geq 1
the differential

\partial\colon\bar{B}_{b}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g))\to\bar{B}_{b-1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)),

is given by the alternating sum
\partial=\sum_{i=0}^{b}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}
, with
\partial_{i}=\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes b-i}
.
Here we denote for simplicity all multiplication maps
by
\mu
, and all identities by
\mathrm{id}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

There is an isomorphism of weighted chain complexes

\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g))\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}.

Proof.

As bigraded abelian group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
is freely generated by the elements
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
for varying record
\mathfrak{t}
of the form
(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
, where
b\geq 0
,
\underline{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{b})
with
P_{i}\geq 1
, and
\mathfrak{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{2g})
with
v_{j}\geq 0
.
Similarly,

\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g))
is freely generated by the elements
1\otimes a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{b}\otimes c
, where
b\geq 0
, each
a_{i}
has the form
x^{\epsilon_{i}}y^{[m_{i}]}
and is different from
1
, and
c
has the form
\prod_{j=1}^{2g}x_{j}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{j}}y_{j}^{[m^{\prime}_{j}]}
, for some
\epsilon_{i},\epsilon^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,1\right\}\mathclose{}
and
m_{i},m^{\prime}_{j}\geq 0
.

Given
\mathfrak{t}=(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
, there are unique decompositions
P_{i}=2m_{i}+\epsilon_{i}
and
v_{j}=2m^{\prime}_{j}+\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}
with
\epsilon_{i},\epsilon^{\prime}_{j},m_{i},m^{\prime}_{j}
as above; setting
a_{i}=x^{\epsilon_{i}}y^{[m_{i}]}
and
c=\prod_{j=1}^{2g}x_{j}^{\epsilon^{\prime}_{j}}y_{j}^{[m^{\prime}_{j}]}
, we obtain a basis element
1\otimes a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{b}\otimes c
of
\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g))
in the same bigrading
(\bullet,\star)=(-\sum_{i=1}^{b}P_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{2g}v_{j},b)
as
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Thus the assignment
e_{\mathfrak{t}}\mapsto 1\otimes a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{b}\otimes c

gives a bigraded bijection of abelian groups

\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g))\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}.

Finally, Proposition
4.14
ensures that the above is a chain map.
∎

The two chain complexes from Proposition
6.1
are concentrated in non-negative bar-degree and non-positive weight, and are of finite rank in each bidegree.

Notation 6.2
.

For a commutative ring
R
we denote by
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(k)
the weighted
R
-algebra
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(k)\otimes R
.
The maps
\mathrm{UMor}(f_{\zeta_{g}})\otimes R
and
\epsilon\otimes R
make

\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(2g)
into a left
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
-module,
and
R
into a
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
-
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
-bimodule.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring; then there is an isomorphism of bigraded
R
-modules

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g);R)\cong H_{\bullet+\star}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R).

Proof.

For
R=\mathbb{Z}
, taking the bigraded dual of the chain complexes from Proposition
6.1
, we obtain an isomorphism of cochain complexes

(6)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g));\mathbb{Z}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{},

where
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
is defined as
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{},\mathbb{Z})
. Here
\mathbb{Z}
is concentrated in bidegree
(\bullet,\star)=(0,0)
, and hence both of the previous cochain complexes are concentrated in non-positive bar-degree and non-negative weight.

On the one hand we identify the cohomology of the first cochain complex

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)),\mathbb{Z}\right)\mathclose{}\cong\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)),\mathbb{Z}\right)\mathclose{}\end{split}

with
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(2g)),\mathbb{Z})
.
On the other hand, by Proposition
2.3
the cohomology of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}
is isomorphic to
H_{\bullet+\star}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Z})
. The convention on bigradings from Notation
4.5
ensures that the isomorphism is bigraded.

For generic
R
, we tensor the isomorphism (
6
) with
R
: the left hand side becomes a cochain complex computing
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g);R)
, and the cohomology of the right hand side is isomorphic by Proposition
2.3
to
H_{\bullet+\star}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
.
∎

6.2.
Yoneda product

In the case
g=0
, we have
\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g)\cong R
, so that Proposition
6.1
, up to identifying
\Sigma_{0,1}
with the disc
D:=[0,1]^{2}
, gives us an isomorphism of bigraded
R
-modules

(7)

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(R,R)\cong H_{\bullet+\star}(C_{\bullet}(D);R).

Both sides admit a structure of bigraded
R
-algebra: the left hand side has a Yoneda product, making it into an associative ring; the right hand side has a Pontryagin product, coming from the
E_{2}
-algebra structure on
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
, and it is thus a graded-commutative ring. Moreover, for any
g\geq 0
, each of the two terms in the isomorphism of Proposition
6.3
is naturally a bigraded left module over one of these rings:
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g),R)
is a left module over
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(R,R)
via the Yoneda product, whereas the
E_{1}
-module structure of
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{g,1})
over the
E_{2}
-algebra
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
makes
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{g,1});R)
into a left module over
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D});R)
. The main result of this subsection is the following proposition.

Proposition 6.4
.

Isomorphism (
7
) is an isomorphism of bigraded rings; after identifying these two rings, the isomorphism from Proposition
6.3
is an isomorphism of bigraded (left) modules.

Definition 6.5
.

Recall Definition
4.1
. We let
\tilde{\theta}\colon D\sqcup\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
be the map sending

\begin{array}[]{cl}(x,y)\mapsto\mathfrak{p}(x,y)&\mbox{for }(x,y)\in D,\\
\mathfrak{p}(x,y)\mapsto\mathfrak{p}(1+x/2,y)&\mbox{for }(x,y)\in\mathbf{R}.\end{array}

The map
\tilde{\theta}
can be used to describe conveniently the
E_{1}
-algebra and module structures of
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
and
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})
, as we discuss in the following.
Since
\tilde{\theta}
restricts to an open embedding
\mathring{D}\sqcup\mathring{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow\mathring{\mathcal{M}}
, it gives rise to a map

C_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})\colon C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})\times C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}});

the map
C_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})
is an open embedding and for
n\geq 0
it restricts to an open embedding

C_{n}(\tilde{\theta})\colon\coprod_{m=0}^{n}C_{m}(\mathring{D})\times C_{n-m}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})\hookrightarrow C_{n}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}).

For
g=0
, the map
C_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})\colon C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})\times C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
is part of the
E_{2}
-algebra structure on
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
, and
for
g\geq 0
the map
C_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})
is part of the
E_{1}
-module structure of
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}})
over
C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D})
.
Taking
R
-homology, we obtain that
C_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})
induces in the two cases the Pontryagin ring multiplication
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D});R)
and the Pontryagin module multiplication
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{D});R)\otimes_{R}H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}});R)\to H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}});R)
.

For
n\geq 0
, the open embedding
C_{n}(\tilde{\theta})
induces a collapse map

{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}\colon{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\to\bigvee_{m=0}^{n}{C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}.

For
0\leq\bar{m}\leq n
let

\pi_{\bar{m},n}\colon\bigvee_{m=0}^{n}{C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\to{C}_{\bar{m}}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-\bar{m}}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}

be the map collapsing all wedge summands except the
\bar{m}
th
.
We can now take reduced cohomology and obtain a description of the Pontryagin module multiplication as the vertical composite on the right of the following diagram: all homology and cohomology groups are with understood coefficients in
R
, tensor products are over
R
, and
-\times-
denotes the cross product in
R
-homology and cohomology; the horizontal isomorphisms are given by Proposition
2.3
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(C_{m}(\mathring{D}))\otimes H_{j}(C_{n-m}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{H}^{2m-i}({C}_{m}(D)^{\infty})\otimes\tilde{H}^{2n-2m-j}({C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i+j}(C_{m}(\mathring{D})\times C_{n-m}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{H}^{2n-i-j}({C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i+j}(C_{n}(\mathring{\mathcal{M}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{H}^{2n-i-j}({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times-}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}(\tilde{\theta})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\left(\pi_{m,n}\circ{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

For
0\leq m\leq n
we consider on
{C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
the smash product cell decomposition, and on
\bigvee_{m=0}^{n}{C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
the wedge cell decomposition.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
n\geq 0
; then the map
{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}
is cellular. For an
n
-record
\mathfrak{t}=(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
as in Definition
4.3
, the induced map on reduced cellular chain complexes

{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}_{*}\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\to\bigoplus_{m=0}^{n}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\otimes\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}_{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}

sends the generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
to the sum
\sum_{i=0}^{b}\epsilon_{i}e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\otimes e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}
, where
\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}=(i,(P_{1},\dots,P_{i}))
,
\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}=(b-i,(P_{i+1},\dots,P_{b}),\mathfrak{v})
, and
\epsilon_{i}=(-1)^{(b-i)\sum_{j=0}^{i}P_{j}}\in\mathopen{}\left\{\pm 1\right\}\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

Observe first that
{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}(s)=\infty
. Let
\infty\neq s\in{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
be a configuration; then
s
is in the image of the map
\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}}\colon\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}}\hookrightarrow{C}_{n}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
, for an
n
-record
\mathfrak{t}
as above. Let
(x_{1},\dots,x_{b})\in\mathring{\Delta}^{b}
be the image of
(\Phi^{\mathfrak{t}})^{-1}(s)\in\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}}
along the projection
\Delta^{\mathfrak{t}}\to\Delta^{b}
. If
x_{i}=\frac{1}{2}
for some
1\leq i\leq b
, then
{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}(s)=\infty
. Otherwise there is
0\leq i\leq b
such that
x_{i}<\frac{1}{2}
and either
i=b
or
x_{i+1}>\frac{1}{2}
; then
{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}(s)
lies in the cell
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\times e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}
, whose dimension is
d(\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l})+d(\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r})=d(\mathfrak{t})
. Hence
{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}
is cellular.

The open embedding
C_{n}(\tilde{\theta})
restricts to an open embedding of
d(\mathfrak{t})
-dimensional manifolds
\coprod_{i=0}^{b}e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\times e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}\hookrightarrow e_{\mathfrak{t}}
, so for
0\leq i\leq b
the degree
[{C}_{n}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}_{*}e_{\mathfrak{t}}:e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\times e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}]
is
\pm 1
.
To determine the sign, we parametrise the cells
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}
,
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}
and
e_{\mathfrak{t}}
by the open polysimplices
\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}
,
\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}
and
\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}}
: then we can identify the restricted embedding
C_{n}(\tilde{\theta})\colon e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\times e_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}\to e_{\mathfrak{t}}
with the composition

\begin{split}\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{l}}\times\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{r}}&=\mathring{\Delta}^{i}\times\prod_{j=1}^{i}\mathring{\Delta}^{P_{j}}\times\mathring{\Delta}^{n-i}\times\prod_{j=i+1}^{b}\mathring{\Delta}^{P_{j}}\times\prod_{j=1}^{2g}\mathring{\Delta}^{v_{j}}\\
&\cong\mathring{\Delta}^{i}\times\mathring{\Delta}^{b-i}\times\prod_{j=1}^{b}\mathring{\Delta}^{P_{j}}\times\prod_{j=1}^{2g}\mathring{\Delta}^{v_{j}}\\
&\overset{\psi_{i}\times\mathrm{id}}{\longrightarrow}\mathring{\Delta}^{b}\times\prod_{j=1}^{b}\mathring{\Delta}^{P_{j}}\times\prod_{j=1}^{2g}\mathring{\Delta}^{v_{j}}=\mathring{\Delta}^{\mathfrak{t}},\end{split}

where the first homeomorphism comes from rearranging the factors and has degree
\epsilon_{i}
, and the map
\psi_{i}\colon\mathring{\Delta}^{i}\times\mathring{\Delta}^{b-i}\to\mathring{\Delta}^{b}
, defined by

((x_{1},\dots,x_{i}),(x_{i+1},\dots,x_{b}))\mapsto(\frac{1}{2}x_{1},\dots,\frac{1}{2}x_{i},\frac{1+x_{i+1}}{2},\dots,\frac{1+x_{b}}{2}),

is an orientation-preserving open embedding.
∎

By Lemma
6.6
the map induced by
{C}_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}
on reduced cellular cochains is the following: given records
\mathfrak{t}_{1}=(i,(P_{1},\dots,P_{i}))
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}=(b-i,(P_{i+1},\dots,P_{b}),\mathfrak{v})
representing cells
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}\subset{C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}
and
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}}\subset{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}
, we can consider the dual generators

e_{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}\ \mbox{ and }\ e_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}}^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}

and their tensor product

e_{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}^{\vee}\otimes e_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}}^{\vee}:=(e_{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}\times e_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}})^{\vee}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{m}(D)^{\infty}\wedge{C}_{n-m}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{};

then
\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\tilde{\theta})^{\infty}\right)\mathclose{}^{*}
sends
e_{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}^{\vee}\otimes e_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}}^{\vee}\mapsto\epsilon e_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\vee}
, where

\mathfrak{t}=(b,(P_{1},\dots,P_{b}),\mathfrak{v})\ \mbox{ and }\ \epsilon=(-1)^{(b-i)\sum_{j=1}^{i}P_{j}}.

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

The isomorphism given in the proof of Proposition
6.1
can be dualised to an isomorphism of cochain complexes

\mathrm{Hom}_{R}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(R,\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g),R\right)\mathclose{}\cong\widetilde{\mathrm{Ch}}^{*}\mathopen{}\left({C}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})^{\infty};R\right)\mathclose{};

under this isomorphism, for a record
\mathfrak{t}=(b,\underline{P},\mathfrak{v})
, the dual generator
e_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\vee}
on the right-hand side corresponds to the dual generator
(1\otimes a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{b}\otimes c)^{\vee}
, where the monomial
a_{i}\in\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)
depends on
P_{i}
, and the monomial
c\in\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g)
depends on
\mathfrak{v}
.
The Yoneda product is induced by the cochain map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{R}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(R,\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1),R),R\right)\mathclose{}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{R}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(R,\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g)),R\right)\mathclose{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{R}\mathopen{}\left(\bar{B}_{\star}(R,\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1),\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g)),R\right)\mathclose{},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\!\otimes a_{1}\otimes\!\dots\!\otimes a_{i}\otimes 1)^{\vee}\!\otimes\!(1\!\otimes a_{i+1}\otimes\!\dots\!\otimes a_{b}\otimes c)^{\vee}\!\mapsto(-1)^{\epsilon}(1\!\otimes a_{1}\otimes\!\dots\!\otimes a_{b}\otimes c)^{\vee},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

where
\epsilon=(-1)^{(b-i)\sum_{j=1}^{i}P_{j}}
is a sign that makes
Y
into a cochain map: here it is important to recall that Koszul signs are computed with respect to the
homological degree
, which is the sum of the weight and the bar-degree. Combining this observation with the discussion after Lemma
6.6
, we obtain the desired statement.
∎

6.3.
The Künneth isomorphism

By Proposition
3.9
, the weighted
R
-algebra
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
is isomorphic to the tensor product over
R
of the algebras
\Lambda_{R}(x)
and
\Gamma_{R}(y)
; we can also consider
R
as an
R
-algebra concentrated in weight 0, and decompose
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
as the tensor product of the algebras
\Lambda_{R}(x)
,
\Gamma_{R}(y)
, and
R
.
Similarly, the
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
-module
R
is isomorphic to the tensor product of the
\Lambda_{R}(x)
-module
R
, the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
R
, and the
R
-module
R
.
Finally, also the
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(1)
-module
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}^{R}(2g)
is isomorphic to the tensor product of the
\Lambda_{R}(x)
-module
R
, the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
, and the
R
-module
\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g})
: indeed by Theorem
3.13
the ring homomorphism
\mathrm{UMor}_{\bullet}(f_{\zeta_{g}})
sends
x\mapsto 0
and sends
y^{[m]}\mapsto\Omega_{2m}\in\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
(see Definition
4.11
).

Definition 6.7
.

For
g\geq 0
we denote by
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}
the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
, with module structure induced by the homomorphism of
R
-algebras
\Gamma_{R}(y)\to\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
sending
y^{[m]}\mapsto\Omega_{2m}
.

Since all algebras and relative modules are free and of finite type when considered as weighted
R
-modules, (i.e. in each weight they coincide with a finitely generated free
R
-module), and since the module
R\cong R\otimes_{R}R\otimes_{R}R
is supported in a single weight, we obtain a Künneth decomposition of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g),R)
as

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Lambda_{R}(x)}(R,R)\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)\otimes_{R}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R).

Proof of Theorem
B
.

The Künneth isomorphism is compatible with the Yoneda product; in particular the
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(R,R)
-module
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(1)}(\mathrm{UMor}^{R}_{\bullet}(2g),R)
is the tensor product of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Lambda_{R}(x)}(R,R)
as a module over itself, the
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(R,R)
-module

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)
, and the
R
-module
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R)
.

We compute explicitly all factors in the previous decomposition and use the grading convention from Remark
1.3
. The ring isomorphism
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Lambda_{R}(x)}(R,R)\cong R[\epsilon]
is standard, so we just comment on bigradings. The class
\epsilon\in\mathrm{Ext}^{-1}_{\Lambda_{R}(x)}(R,R)
, represented by
(1\otimes x\otimes 1)^{\vee}
, has bar-degree
\star=-1
. Since
x
has weight
-1
,
\epsilon
has weight
\bullet=-(-1)=1
. Hence the homological degree of
\epsilon
is
*=\bullet+\star=1-1=0
.

The second factor
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)
is the same presented in the statement of Theorem
B
, and we will analyse it more closely later; for the moment, notice that for
g=0
we have
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}=\Lambda_{R}()\cong R
, as in (1) of Theorem
B
.

The third factor
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R)
is concentrated in bar-degree
\star=0
, as
\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g})
is free, hence projective over
R
. We are thus interested in computing

\mathrm{Ext}^{0}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R)=\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R).

Let
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}
denote the
R
-linear dual of
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g}Ry_{i}
; since each
y_{i}
has weight
-2
,
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}
is concentrated in weight
\bullet=2
; we put it in bar-degree
\star=0
. The free
divided power
R
-algebra

\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{g})
has a natural coproduct given on generators by
\Delta(y_{i}^{[m]})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{i}^{[j]}\otimes y_{i}^{[m-j]}
, and making
\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{g})
into a
R
-Hopf algebra. The
R
-linear dual

\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{g}),R)
is also an
R
-Hopf algebra, and as an algebra it agrees with the free
symmetric
R
-algebra
on
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}
. We thus identify
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{R}(\Gamma_{R}(y_{1},\dots,y_{2g}),R)
with
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}]
as
R
-modules.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

The fact that the
R
-module
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}]
has a natural algebra structure suggests that there should be an action of
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}]
on
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});R)
. Such an action
is sketched in
[
19
, Subsection 1.1]
.

6.4.
Direct sum splitting of
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}

In this subsection we decompose the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R}
as a direct sum of smaller modules, thus reducing the computation of the term
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g}^{R},R)
from Theorem
B
to smaller computations.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
u\geq 0
. We consider
B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}:=\mathbb{Z}[z_{1},\dots,z_{u}]/(z_{1}^{2},\dots,z_{u}^{2})
as a weighted ring, by putting each
z_{i}
in weight
\bullet=-2
. We let
\phi_{1}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)\to B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
be the unique ring homomorphism sending

y\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{u}z_{i}
; we have
\phi_{1}\colon y^{[m]}\mapsto\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{m}\leq u}z_{i_{1}}\dots z_{i_{m}}
.
This makes
B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
into a
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)
-algebra, and in particular into a
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)
-module.

We will also consider the unique ring homomorphism
\phi_{2}\colon\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)\to B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
, sending
\phi_{2}\colon y\mapsto 2\sum_{i=1}^{u}z_{i}
; we have
\phi_{2}(y^{[m]})=2^{m}\phi_{1}(y^{[m]})
. We denote by
2\!B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
the corresponding
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)
-module structure on the weighted abelian group
B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
, to distinguish it from the module structure induced by
\phi_{1}
.

For a generic ring
R
we let
B^{R}_{u}
and
2\!B^{R}_{u}
be the corresponding
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules, obtained from the previous by tensoring with
R
.

Notation 6.10
.

Let
\mathbf{K}=\mathopen{}\left\{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}\right\}\mathclose{}
be the set with three elements
\mathbf{d},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{e}
; for a function
\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}
we denote by
\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e})\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}
the preimage of
\mathbf{e}
and we let
0\leq|\xi|\leq g
be the cardinality of
\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e})
.

Proposition 6.11
.

For
g\geq 0
, we have an isomorphism of weighted
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules

\mathbb{M}^{R}_{g}\cong\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}}}2\!B^{R}_{|\xi|}\ [|\xi|-g],

where
2\!B^{R}_{r}[-m]
is the weighted
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
2\!B^{R}_{r}
shifted down by
m
.

Proof.

The ring homomorphism
\Gamma_{R}(y)\to\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
sending
y^{[m]}\mapsto\Omega_{2m}
(and making

\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
into a
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module) can be written as the composite of the ring homomorphism
\phi_{2}\colon\Gamma_{R}(y)\to B^{R}_{g}
(see Definition
6.9
) and the ring homomorphism
B^{R}_{g}\to\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
sending
z_{i}\mapsto x_{2i-1}\wedge x_{2i}
. Thus we can lift the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module structure on
\mathbb{M}^{R}_{g}
to a
B^{R}_{g}
-module structure.

For
\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}
and
1\leq i\leq g
let
x_{\xi,i}\in\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
be:

x_{\xi,i}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}x_{2i-1}&\mbox{ if }\xi(i)=\mathbf{d};\\[1.99997pt]
x_{2i}&\mbox{ if }\xi(i)=\mathbf{u};\\[1.99997pt]
1&\mbox{ if }\xi(i)=\mathbf{e}.\end{array}\right.

Let
x_{\xi}=x_{\xi,1}\wedge\dots\wedge x_{\xi,g}\in\Lambda^{g-|\xi|}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
, and note that
x_{\xi}
has weight
|\xi|-g
. For
\emptyset\subseteq S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}
let
z_{S}=\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}\in B^{R}_{g}
. Note that
z_{S}\cdot x_{\xi}=0
if
S\nsubseteq\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e})
. Instead, the elements
z_{S}\cdot x_{\xi}
, for varying
\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}
and
\emptyset\subseteq S\subseteq\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e})
, give a basis of
\Lambda_{R}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2g})
as a free
R
-module. Hence
\mathbb{M}^{R}_{g}
decomposes as a
B^{R}_{g}
-module (and hence as a
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module) as a direct sum

\mathbb{M}^{R}_{g}\cong\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}}}B^{R}_{g}\cdot x_{\xi}.

The summand
B^{R}_{g}\cdot x_{\xi}
is isomorphic, as a
B^{R}_{g}
-module, to the shifted quotient
B^{R}_{g}/(z_{i}\ |\ i\notin\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e}))\ [|\xi|-g]
, hence it is isomorphic over
\Gamma_{R}(y)
to
2\!B^{R}_{|\xi|}\ [|\xi|-g]
.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
6.11
, we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g},R)\cong\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}}}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(2\!B^{R}_{|\xi|},R)\ [g-|\xi|],

where again
[m]
shifts up the weight
\bullet
by
m
, without changing the bar-degree
\star
.

6.5.
Proof of the corollaries of Theorem
B

Proof of Corollary
B.1
.

For point (1), split injectivity of
\epsilon\cdot-
follows from the presence of the tensor factor
R[\epsilon]
in part (2) of Theorem
B
; homological stability follows from the following facts:

•

R[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}]
is supported in weights
\bullet\geq 0
and bar-degree
\star=0
;

•

\mathbb{M}_{g}
and
\Gamma_{R}(y)
are supported in non-positive weights,
\mathbb{M}_{g}
is free over
R
and
\Gamma_{R}(y)
is
R
in weight
0
and is otherwise concentrated in weights at most
-2
; as a consequence
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{R}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g},R)
is supported in bidegrees
(\bullet,\star)
satisfying
\bullet\geq-2\star\geq 0
, i.e.
2(\bullet+\star)\geq\bullet
.

Point (2) follows from part (3) of Theorem
B
after noticing the following. First, we have
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(y)\cong\mathbb{Q}[y]
; since
\mathbb{M}_{g}
is finite-dimensional over
\mathbb{Q}
, it splits over
\mathbb{Q}[y]
as a finite direct sum of torsion
\mathbb{Q}[y]
-modules; it follows that

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(y)}(\mathbb{M}_{g},\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{Q}[y]}(\mathbb{M}_{g},\mathbb{Q})

is finite dimensional. Second,
\epsilon
has bigrading
(-1,1)
, so no power of
\epsilon
higher than
\epsilon^{i}
can occur in a class in
H_{k-i}(C_{k}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
: therefore the weighted
\mathbb{Q}[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{\mathbb{Q}}]
-module
H_{\bullet-i}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
is finitely generated. We apply the theory of the Hilbert function for finitely generated weighted modules over a weighted polynomial ring.
∎

Proof of Corollary
B.2
.

The filled genus stabilisation corresponds in the light of Theorem
B
to the tensor product over
R
of the following split injective maps of
R
-modules (whence it is itself split injective):

•

the identity of
R[\epsilon]
;

•

the
R
-split inclusion
R[\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}]\hookrightarrow R[\mathcal{H}_{g+1}^{R}]
given by the
R
-split inclusion
\mathcal{H}_{g}^{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{g+1}^{R}
, which is the
R
-dual of the
R
-split surjection

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g+2}Ry_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g}Ry_{i}
killing
y_{2g+1}
and
y_{2g+2}
;

•

the
R
-split inclusion on Ext groups induced by the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-split surjective map of modules
\mathbb{M}_{g+1}^{R}\to\mathbb{M}_{g}\otimes_{R}\mathopen{}\left(Rx_{2g+1}\oplus Rx_{2g+2}\right)\mathclose{}
which, in the light of Proposition
6.11
, is the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-split surjective map

\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g+1\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}}}\hskip 6.0pt2\!B^{R}_{|\xi|}\ [|\xi|-g]\longrightarrow\hskip 6.0pt\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g+1\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K},\\
|\xi^{-1}(\mathbf{e})\cap\mathopen{}\left\{2g+1,2g+2\right\}\mathclose{}|=1\end{subarray}}}\hskip 6.0pt2\!B^{R}_{|\xi|}\ [|\xi|-g]

killing all summands corresponding to maps
\xi\colon\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,g+1\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathbf{K}
such that either both or neither of
2g+1,2g+2
is sent to
\mathbf{e}
.

∎

Part III
Solution of the Ext problem

In this part of the article we conclude the computation of
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a module over
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{F}_{p})
, for
p
an odd prime. We observe that Theorem
B
and Proposition
6.11
reduce this to computing
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(2\!B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u},\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a module over
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
. We observe that for
p
an odd prime we have an isomorphism of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
-modules
2\!B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u}\cong B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u}
, so we might compute
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u},\mathbb{F}_{p})
as well.

Recall that for any prime
p
(also for
p=2
) we have an identification of rings

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\bigotimes_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p}).

Theorem C
.

Let
p
be a prime, let
u\geq 0
, and let
h\geq 0
be such that

p^{h}-1\leq u\leq p^{h+1}-2.

Then for every
0\leq i\leq h
there is a finitely generated, weighted
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})
-module
N_{u,i}
, such that

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u},\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic, as a bigraded
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
-module, to the direct sum

\bigoplus_{i=0}^{h}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(N_{u,i},\mathbb{F}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\bigotimes_{j=i+1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{j}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{j}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})\right)\mathclose{}.

Here the
i
th
summand is the tensor product of the following modules:

•

\mathbb{F}_{p}
as the augmentation module over the ring
\bigotimes_{j=0}^{i-1}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{j}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{j}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
;

•

\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(N_{u,i},\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a module over
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
;

•

the ring
\bigotimes_{j=i+1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{j}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{j}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a module over itself.

A characterisation of
N_{u,i}
as a finite direct sum of cyclic
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})
-modules will be given in Lemma
9.3
. For a cyclic
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})
-module
N
we consider the explicit computation of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(N,\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a module over
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{p})}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
to be a standard exercise in homological algebra, which we leave to the reader.

In Section
7
we will analyse more closely the
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module
B^{R}_{u}
, for a generic commutative ring
R
. For
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, these results will be used in Section
8
to check that
B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u}
is a
tame
module over
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\dots]/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{p},\dots)
(see Definition
8.5
).
We will then prove a structure result for tame modules, allowing in particular a computation of
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(B^{\mathbb{F}_{p}}_{u},\mathbb{F}_{p})
as a
\mathrm{Ext}^{\star}_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)}(\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
-module.

7.
Analysis of
B^{R}_{u}

In this section we fix
u\geq 0
and a commutative ring
R
, and work in the category
\mathrm{Mod}_{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of weighted
R
-modules.
Abstractly,
B^{R}_{u}
is isomorphic to the cohomology ring of
(\mathbb{C}P^{1})^{u}
with coefficients in
R
.
The results that we obtain in the following correspond, in the case
R=\mathbb{Q}
, to (almost all of) the “Kähler package” of the rational cohomology of
(\mathbb{C}P^{1})^{u}
, considered as a projective subvariety of
\mathbb{C}P^{2^{u}-1}
via the standard Segre embedding (so that
y\in B^{\mathbb{Q}}_{u}
corresponds to the class of a hyperplane in
\mathbb{C}P^{2^{u}-1}
). We shall use a corresponding terminology.

Notation 7.1
.

For
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}
we denote by
z_{S}
the element
\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}\in B^{R}_{u}
.

Recall Definition
6.9
, and note that the ring homomorphism
\phi_{1}\colon\Gamma_{R}(y)\to B^{R}_{u}
sends
y^{[k]}=y^{k}/k!\mapsto\sum_{S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{},|S|=k}z_{S}
, for all
k\geq 0
. In particular, for
S^{\prime}\subset\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}
, we have
y^{[k]}\cdot z_{S^{\prime}}=\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{},|S|=|S^{\prime}|+k}z_{S}
.

Notation 7.2
.

Let
R
be a ring (concentrated in weight 0) and let
M
be a weighted
R
-module. We denote by
M^{\vee}
the weighted
R
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(M,R)
; if
M(n)
is the part of
M
of weight
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
M^{\vee}
has

\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(M(n),R)
in weight
-n
. We leave
R
implicit in the notation.
For a weighted
R
-algebra
A
and a weighted
A
-module
M
, we can consider
M
as an
A
-
R
-bimodule, and thus consider
M^{\vee}
as an
A
-module.

Lemma 7.3

(Poincaré duality)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules

(B^{R}_{u})^{\vee}\cong B^{R}_{u}[2u].

Proof.

A weighted
R
-linear basis for
B^{R}_{u}
is given by the monomials
z_{S}
. Denote by
\bar{S}
the complement of
S
, and by
z_{S}^{\vee}\in(B^{R}_{u})^{\vee}=\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(B^{R}_{u},R)
the map sending
z_{S}\mapsto 1
and
z_{S^{\prime}}\mapsto 0
for
S^{\prime}\neq S
. Note that
z_{S}^{\vee}\in(B^{R}_{u})^{\vee}
has weight
2|S|
. The
R
-linear map
B^{R}_{u}[2u]\to(B^{R}_{u})^{\vee}
sending
z_{S}[2u]\mapsto z^{\vee}_{\bar{S}}
is a
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-linear isomorphism.
∎

Notation 7.4
.

Let
A
be a weighted algebra and
M
a weighted
A
-module. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we denote by
M_{\geq k}\subseteq M
the sub-
A
-module by the elements of weight at least
k
, and by
M_{\leq k}
the sub-
A
-module generated by the elements of weight at most
k
.

Note that if
A
is concentrated in non-negative weights, then
M_{\leq k}
is just the additive span of all elements of
M
of weight at most
k
.

Example 7.5
.

For
k\geq 0
, we have that
B^{R}_{u,\geq-2k}\subset B^{R}_{u}
is the sub-
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module generated by all elements
z_{S}
with
|S|\leq k
(i.e.
z_{S}
has weight at least
-2k
), whereas
B^{R}_{u,\leq-2k}\subset B^{R}_{u}
is the sub-
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module generated by all elements
z_{S}
with
|S|\geq k
(i.e.
z_{S}
has weight at most
-2k
).
Note that
B^{R}_{u,\leq-2k}
coincides with the
R
-linear span of the elements
z_{S}
with
|S|\geq k
.
For instance,
B^{R}_{u,\leq-2u}
is spanned by the single monomial
z_{1}\dots z_{u}
, and
B^{R}_{u,\geq 0}
is spanned by the elements
\sum_{|S|=k}z_{S}
for varying
0\leq k\leq u
. On the other hand, we have

B^{R}_{u,\leq 0}=B^{R}_{u,\geq-2u}=B^{R}_{u}
.

Lemma 7.6

(Hard Lefschetz)
.

Let
0\leq k\leq u
. Then
B^{R}_{u,\geq-u+k}
contains
B^{R}_{u,\leq-u-k}
.

Proof.

Let
T\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}
have cardinality at least
(u+k)/2
, so that
z_{T}
is a generator of
B^{R}_{u,\leq-u-k}
, and let
\bar{T}
be the complement of
T
. Then the inclusion-exclusion principle implies that
z_{T}
can be expressed as

z_{T}=\sum_{S\subseteq\bar{T}}(-1)^{|S|}\ y^{[|T|-|S|]}\cdot z_{S},

and in particular
z_{T}\in B^{R}_{u,\geq-u+k}
.
∎

Definition 7.7
.

A finite subset
S\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is
sparse
if for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have
|S\cap\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,i\right\}\mathclose{}|\leq|S\setminus\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,i\right\}\mathclose{}|
, or equivalently
|S\cap\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,i\right\}\mathclose{}|\leq\frac{i}{2}
.
We let

\Theta_{u}\colon\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}\\[1.00374pt]
S\ \mathrm{sparse}\end{subarray}}}\Gamma_{R}(y)\left<X_{S}\right>\to B^{R}_{u}

be the map of weighted
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules sending
X_{S}\mapsto z_{S}
, where
X_{S}
is a free generator in weight
-2|S|
.

We note that if
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}
is sparse, then in particular
|S|\leq\lfloor u/2\rfloor
.

Notation 7.8
.

For
u\geq 0
and
0\leq k\leq\lfloor u/2\rfloor
, we denote
\ell_{u,k}=\binom{u}{k}-\binom{u}{k-1}>0
, where we set
\binom{u}{-1}=0
.

Lemma 7.9
.

For
u\geq 0
and
0\leq k\leq\lfloor u/2\rfloor
there are precisely
\ell_{u,k}
sparse subsets
|S|\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}
of cardinality
k
.

Proof.

We leave the proof to the reader.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

The map
\Theta_{u}
is surjective.

Proof.

We induct on
u\geq 0
. For
r=0
the map
\Theta_{0}\colon\Gamma_{R}(y)\left<X_{\emptyset}\right>\to B^{R}_{0}\cong R
sends
X_{\emptyset}\mapsto z_{\emptyset}=1
, so it is surjective.
For the inductive step, we assume
u\geq 1
and note that there is a short exact sequence of
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-modules

(8)

z_{u}B^{R}_{u}\hookrightarrow B^{R}_{u}\twoheadrightarrow B^{R}_{u}/z_{u},

and there are
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-isomorphisms
B^{R}_{u-1}[-2]\cong z_{u}B^{R}_{u}
, sending
z_{S}[-2]\mapsto z_{u}z_{S}
, for
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,r-1\right\}\mathclose{}
, and
B^{R}_{u-1}\cong B^{R}_{u}/z_{u}
, sending
z_{S}\mapsto[z_{S}]_{/z_{u}}
.

By the inductive hypothesis and (
8
), the following elements suffice, together, to generate the entire
B^{R}_{u}
over
\Gamma_{R}(y)
:

•

the elements
z_{S}
, lifting
[z_{S}]_{/z_{u}}
, for
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u-1\right\}\mathclose{}
sparse;

•

the elements
z_{S\cup\mathopen{}\left\{u\right\}\mathclose{}}=z_{u}z_{S}
, for
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u-1\right\}\mathclose{}
sparse.

Suppose now that
S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u-1\right\}\mathclose{}
is sparse but
S\cup\mathopen{}\left\{u\right\}\mathclose{}
is not sparse. Then the failure of
S\cup\mathopen{}\left\{u\right\}\mathclose{}
from being sparse must be witnessed by the inequality
|S|>|\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}\setminus S|
, that is
|S|>\lfloor u/2\rfloor
; by Lemma
7.6
we can safely remove the element
z_{S\cup\mathopen{}\left\{u\right\}\mathclose{}}=z_{u}z_{S}
from the above list and still be left with a list of generators of
B^{R}_{u}
.
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

The map
\Theta_{u}
is injective in weights at least
-2(u-\lfloor u/2\rfloor)
. More generally, for all
0\leq k\leq\lfloor u/2\rfloor
, the map
\Theta_{u}
restricts to a map

\Theta_{u,k}\colon\bigoplus_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,u\right\}\mathclose{}\\[1.00374pt]
|S|\leq k,\ S\ \mathrm{sparse}\end{subarray}}}\Gamma_{R}(y)\left<X_{S}\right>\to B^{R}_{u}

that is split injective (as a map of
R
-modules) in every weight
-2m\geq-2(u-k)
. In particular
B^{R}_{u,\geq-2k}
, that is the image of
\Theta_{u,k}
, agrees with a free
\Gamma_{R}(y)
-module in weights at least
-2(u-k)
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the result for the case
R=\mathbb{Z}
: for general
R
, we notice that all modules and maps are basechanged from
\mathbb{Z}
, and basechange preserves split injectivity.

Let
k\leq\lfloor u/2\rfloor
, and let
m
be either at most
k
or equal to
u-k
. By Lemma
7.9
, in weight
-2m
both the source and the target of
\Theta_{u,k}
are free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules of same rank
\binom{r}{m}
. Moreover, by Lemmas
7.6
and
7.10
, the map

\Theta_{u,k}
is surjective in weight
-2m
. It follows that
\Theta_{u,k}
is an isomorphism in this weight, and in particular it is split injective.

Consider now a weight
-2m
for
k<m<u-k
. We can consider the surjective
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Z}}(y)
-map
\mathfrak{q}_{u,m+k}\colon B^{R}_{u}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{m+k}
obtained by quotienting the variables
z_{m+k+1},\dots,z_{u}
in the ring
B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{u}
. The composition
\mathfrak{q}_{u,m+k}\circ\Theta_{u,k}
coincides with
\Theta_{m+k,k}
, and in particular it restricts to an isomorphism, and in particular a split injection, in weight
-2m
by the previous argument. It follows that
\Theta_{u,k}
is split injective in weight
-2m
.
∎

Remark 7.12
.

Putting together the results of this section, and taking
R=\mathbb{Q}
, one obtains that
B^{\mathbb{Q}}_{u}
is isomorphic, as a module over the ring
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(y)\cong\mathbb{Q}[t]
, to the direct sum

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\lfloor u/2\rfloor}\mathopen{}\left(\mathbb{Q}[y]/(y^{u+1-2k})[-2k]\right)\mathclose{}^{\bigoplus\ell_{u,k}}.

In particular, we obtain the analogue of Theorem
C
over
\mathbb{Q}
, and together with Theorem
B
, we obtain a computation of
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(\Sigma_{g,1});\mathbb{Q})
as a
H_{*}(C_{\bullet}(D);\mathbb{Q})
-module.

8.
Tame modules

In the entire section we fix a field
\mathbb{F}
and work in the category
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of weighted
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces; all tensor products are assumed to be over
\mathbb{F}
.

Notation 8.1
.

For
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\mathopen{}\left\{\pm\infty\right\}\mathclose{}
with
m<m^{\prime}
, we denote by
{]m,m^{\prime}[}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
the set of all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
with
m<i<m^{\prime}
. Similarly, if
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
[m,m^{\prime}]=\mathopen{}\left\{i\in\mathbb{Z}\,|\,m\leq i\leq m^{\prime}\right\}\mathclose{}
, and we define analogously
[m,m^{\prime}[
and
]m,m^{\prime}]
.

8.1.
Definition of tame modules and examples

Definition 8.2
.

Let
\underline{d}=(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2},\dots)
be a sequence of positive integers with
d_{0}\geq 1
and
d_{i}\geq 2
for
i\geq 1
.
We denote by
\mathcal{A}(\underline{d})
the truncated polynomial algebra

\mathcal{A}(\underline{d})=\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1},\mathfrak{y}_{2},\dots]/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{d_{1}},\mathfrak{y}_{1}^{d_{2}},\mathfrak{y}_{2}^{d_{3}},\dots)=\bigotimes_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{d_{i+1}}),

where
\mathfrak{y}_{i}
is a variable of weight
-2\prod_{j=0}^{i}d_{j}
.

Notation 8.3
.

We fix the sequence
\underline{d}
throughout the section, and abbreviate
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\underline{d})
. We also set
D_{i}=\prod_{j=0}^{i}d_{j}
for all
i\geq-1
, so that
\mathfrak{y}_{i}
has weight
-2D_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
; in particular
D_{0}=d_{0}
and
D_{-1}=1
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{0}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{d_{1}})
the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
generated by the first variable
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
, and by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
the “complementary” subalgebra
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{i}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{i}^{d_{i+1}})
. Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},\dots)
.

Note that
\mathcal{A}
is a weighted
\mathbb{F}
-algebra concentrated in non-positive weights: more precisely,
\mathcal{A}
has dimension 1 over
\mathbb{F}
in each non-positive weight multiple of
2d_{0}
, and dimension 0 in every different weight. Moreover
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Example 8.4
.

If
p
is a prime number, we can set
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
d_{0}=1
and
d_{i}=p
for all
i\geq 1
: then
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\underline{d})
is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
, with
\mathfrak{y}_{i}
corresponding to
y^{[i]}
.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
A
be a weighted algebra concentrated in non-positive weights. A weighted
A
-module
M
is
tame
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

M
is finite dimensional as an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space;

•

M\cong M^{\vee}
as
A
-modules;

•

for all
k\geq 0
,
M_{\geq k}
(see Notation
7.4
) agrees in weights at least
-k
with a free
A
-module. In other words, there exists a free
A
-module
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, generated in weights at least
k
, and an
A
-module homomorphism
\psi_{k}\colon\mathcal{F}_{k}\to M
which is bijective in weights
[k,\infty[
and injective in weights
[-k,k]
.

For
u\in\mathbb{Z}
,
M
is
u
-tame
over
A
if the shifted module
M[u]
is tame over
A
.

We note that if
M
is tame over
A
and
A^{\prime}\subset A
is a sub-
\mathbb{F}
-algebra such that
A
is free as an
A^{\prime}
-module, then
M
is also tame over
A^{\prime}
.

Example 8.6
.

If
A
is concentrated in non-positive weights and is
\mathbb{F}
in weight 0 (generated by the unit), then the augmentation module
\mathbb{F}
, i.e. the quotient of
A
by its elements of negative weight, is a tame
A
-module.

Notation 8.7
.

In the setting of Example
8.6
, we denote by
A_{+}\subset A
the augmentation ideal of
A
, spanned by all elements of negative weight.

Example 8.8
.

For all
i\geq 0
, the shifted quotient
\mathcal{A}/(\mathfrak{y}_{i},\mathfrak{y}_{i+1},\dots)[D_{i}-d_{0}]
is a tame
\mathcal{A}
-module: it is generated by the single element
1
(in weight
D_{i}-d_{0}
), and it coincides with the quotient of
\mathcal{A}[D_{i}-d_{0}]
by all elements of weight at most
-D_{i}+d_{0}
.

Example 8.9
.

Let
p
be a prime number, let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and let
u\geq 0
. Then Lemma
7.3
and Proposition
7.11
imply that
B^{p}_{u}[u]
is tame as a module over the algebra
\mathcal{A}\cong\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(y)
from Example
8.4
; in other words,
B^{p}_{u}
is
u
-tame.

Remark 8.10
.

Let
M
be a tame
A
-module, let
k\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
be as in Definition
8.5
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{k}
must have in each weight
m\geq k
as many free
A
-generators as the dimension of
M/A_{+}M
in weight
m
: this characterises
\mathcal{F}_{k}
up to isomorphism.
Moreover, let
\psi_{k},\tilde{\psi}_{k}\colon\mathcal{F}_{k}\to M
be two
A
-linear maps inducing a bijection in weights at least
k
; then both
\psi_{k}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{k}
induce a bijection
\mathcal{F}_{k}/A_{+}\mathcal{F}_{k}\overset{\cong}{\to}M/A_{+}M
in weights at least
k
, and the composite bijection
\mathcal{F}_{k}/A_{+}\mathcal{F}_{k}\overset{\cong}{\to}M/A_{+}M\overset{\cong}{\leftarrow}\mathcal{F}_{k}/A_{+}\mathcal{F}_{k}
can be lifted to an
A
-linear automorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
intertwining
\psi_{k}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{k}
. In particular
\psi_{k}
is injective in weights in
[-k,k]
if and only if
\tilde{\psi}_{k}
is injective in this range of weights.

8.2.
Barcodes

Recall Notation
8.3
. If
M
is a
\mathcal{A}
-module, it is in particular a
\mathcal{R}
-module; since
\mathcal{A}
is free over
\mathcal{R}
, if
M
is tame over
\mathcal{A}
then it is also tame over
\mathcal{R}
. The ring
\mathcal{R}
is a quotient of the PID
\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{0}]
, and a finitely generated, weighted
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of shifts of cyclic modules, of the form
\mathbb{F}[\mathfrak{y}_{0}]/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{c})[m]
for some
1\leq c\leq d_{1}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 8.11
.

The
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
-
barcode
of a finitely generated, weighted
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
is the finite multiset
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{y}_{0}}(M)
of pairs
(m,c)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathopen{}\left\{1,\dots,d_{1}\right\}\mathclose{}
indexing the direct sum
\bigoplus_{(m,c)}\mathcal{R}/\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{c}[m]
classifying the isomorphism type of
M
as an
\mathcal{R}
-module.

Each pair
(m,c)
is a
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
-
bar
; the number
c
is the
size
of the bar, and it coincides with the dimension over
\mathbb{F}
of
\mathcal{R}/(\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{c})[m]
;
the support of
(m,c)
is the finite subset
\mathopen{}\left\{m-2id_{0}\,|\,0\leq i\leq c-1\right\}\mathclose{}\subset\mathbb{Z}
. The
barycentre
of
(m,c)
is the integer
m-(c-1)d_{0}
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
M
be a
u
-tame
\mathcal{R}
-module;
then the barcode
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{y}_{0}}(M)

satisfies the following:

(1)

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and each
1\leq c\leq d_{1}
, there are as many bars of size
c
with barycentre
-u-i
as there are with barycentre
-u+i
;

(2)

each bar
(m,c)
of size
1\leq c<d_{1}
has barycentre
inside
]{-u-d_{0}},{-u+d_{0}}[
.

Proof.

We only consider the case
u=0
.
Part (1) is a direct consequence of the
\mathcal{R}
-linear isomorphism
M\cong M^{\vee}
.
For part (2), let
(m,c)
be a bar in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{y}_{0}}(M)
with
1\leq c<d_{1}
;
by (1) we can assume

m\geq(c-1)d_{0}
that is,

(m,c)
has barycentre at least
0
. We now assume

m\geq cd_{0}
, that is, the barycentre is at least
d_{0}
,
and look for a contradiction.
Decompose of
M
as a direct sum of cyclic
\mathcal{R}
-modules, and let
\nu\in M
be a generator of a summand corresponding to the bar
(m,c)
;
then
\nu\in M_{\geq m}
, which agrees with a free
\mathcal{R}
-module in weights at least
-m
; since
\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{c}\nu=0
is an equality in
M_{\geq m}
in weight
m-2cd_{0}\geq-m
, we must have that
\nu
is divisible by
\mathfrak{y}_{0}^{d_{1}-c}
, hence by
\mathfrak{y}_{0}
, in
M_{\geq m}
and hence also in
M
. But
\nu
generates a cyclic summand of
M
.
∎

8.3.
The free-narrow decomposition

Definition 8.13
.

A graded vector space is
narrow
if it is concentrated in weights in
]{-D_{1}+d_{0}-u},{D_{1}-d_{0}-u}[
.

Proposition 8.14
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
M
be a
u
-tame
\mathcal{A}
-module (see Notation
8.3
).
Then
M
is isomorphic, as an
\mathcal{A}
-module, to the direct sum
F\oplus N
of two
u
-tame
\mathcal{A}
-modules
F
and
N
, such that
F
is free over the subring
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{A}
, and
N
is narrow.
The isomorphism types of
F
and
N
as
\mathcal{A}
-modules are uniquely determined by
M
.

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
8.14
; we only consider the case
